(3) Percentage of population\nliving on less than $3.65 a day at 2017 international prices.\nExecutive Summary\nAndrés Manuel López Obrador (commonly called AMLO) promised a Fourth Transformation – a\nrevolution against insecurity, corruption and inequality. He pledged to reduce the military’s role\nin the drug war and to confront the root causes of violence. In practice, however, his administration\nhas not deviated much from the practices of previous presidencies and has continued to pursue a\nneoliberal economic agenda. Corruption continues to be rampant, social inequalities persist, and\nthe war on drugs continues to have heavy military involvement and claim lives.\nDrug cartels continue to hold significant influence in politics and dominate some territorial areas.\nThe president has been criticized for having a practically nonexistent security policy. There are\neven indications that the recent subnational electoral victories of MORENA, the president’s party,\nwere supported by criminal groups. Instead of reducing the militarization of the drug war, AMLO\nhas expanded the military’s role in internal security and transferred control of the National Guard\naway from civilian hands to the army, which runs counter to the constitution and is being\ndeliberated by the courts. The military’s role has also extended into economic activities (e.g., the\nconstruction of the Mayan Train, customs, ports, etc.). For the first time in modern Mexican\nhistory, the army holds real power, which could eventually threaten civilian authorities.\nAMLO is well-known for attacking critics and continues to pursue a political strategy of\nconsolidating power in the executive branch. Although MORENA and its satellite parties lost their\nsupermajority in the 2021 legislative election, they still maintain a simple majority, which allows\nthem to pass legislation with the exception of constitutional reforms. The government is impeding\naccountability institutions through takeovers and funding reductions, or even attempts to eliminate\nthem entirely. In late 2022, Congress approved AMLO’s “Plan B,” which diminishes the resources\ndevoted to election oversight institutions and significantly impairs their effectiveness. The\nSupreme Court is currently deliberating legal appeals against the reform package. In recent years,\nthe Supreme Court, which includes four of AMLO’s nominees, has consistently ruled in favor of\nthe government. However, this could change with the newly appointed Supreme Court president,\nwho appears to be independent.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 4\nThe president also continues to attack critical journalists on a daily basis and is highly suspicious\nof autonomous organizations. He often clashes with anti-corruption groups, human rights\norganizations and women’s groups (among other associations) and has pulled funds from CSOs,\nwhile threatening to abolish some and integrate their functions. It was expected that the leftist\nMORENA movement would support the politically left-leaning demands of Indigenous people,\nwomen, the LGTBQ+ movement and environmentalists; however, the presidency considers these\ngroups to be adversaries. While AMLO’s actions have eroded democratic life, he continues to\nenjoy enduring popularity, with approval levels above 60%.\nEconomically, except for macroeconomic measures guided by the Bank of Mexico and the\nSecretariat of the Treasury and Public Credit, the president’s investment decisions are guided by\npolitical motives. The government directly awards most major public project contracts to the army\nor national enterprises without public competition. The economy has not shown improvement\nunder AMLO’s leadership. Throughout the COVID-19 pandemic, Mexico pursued a strict path of\nausterity in order to avoid accumulating budget deficits. Nevertheless, public debt increased by 5\npercentage points between 2019 and 2021, the budget deficit rose to nearly 4% of GDP in 2022,\nand the account balance had deteriorated significantly by August 2022. The country is not expected\nto recover to the 2019 GDP level until late 2022 or early 2023. Additionally, although AMLO\nprofesses to pursue an anti-neoliberal economic agenda, capital freedom remains intact in all\neconomic sectors except for the energy sector, and Mexico entered into a new free trade agreement,\nsimilar to NAFTA, with the United States and Canada.\nHistory and Characteristics of Transformation\nThe enduring characteristic of the Mexican state from 1929 to 2000 was the existence of a\nhegemonic political party – the PRI – that controlled most social organizations. The PRI regime\nemerged from the Mexican Revolution (1910 – 1917) and, drawing on the 1917 constitution,\nleveraged the symbolic power of the revolution as a source of its legitimacy and justification for\nits wide-ranging intervention in the economy. The three decades following the beginning of World\nWar II were a golden age for Mexico, known as the Mexican Miracle. However, by the end of the\n1960s, economic momentum began to falter, and public discontent with the political regime grew.\nThe student movement manifesting this discontent was subject to violent suppression between\n1968 and 1971.\nIn the 1970s, economic prosperity came to an end as the import substitution model reached its\nlimits, culminating in the debt crisis of 1982. The financial crisis, coupled with pressure from\ninternational financial institutions, led Miguel de la Madrid’s government (1982 – 1988) to\nliberalize the economy. The following governments (1988 – 2000) continued this economic\ncourse. Significant steps toward liberalization included the signing of the North American Free\nTrade Agreement (NAFTA) and Mexico’s admission to the OECD in 1994. The 1994 Zapatista\nuprising symbolized the rejection of these policies by those who were to lose out from the\nneoliberal economic strategy. Increasing discontent with the PRI’s single-party rule fueled support\nfor opposition parties in the 2000 elections – the right-wing National Action Party (PAN) and the\nleft-wing Party of the Democratic Revolution (PRD).\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 5\nRiding the wave of anti-PRI frustration, PAN candidate Vicente Fox won the 2000 presidential\nelection. Although his victory drew expectations of change, Fox failed to establish more\ndemocratic institutions and continued to apply the orthodox liberal economic model. His\ngovernment’s social assistance policies did not significantly reduce poverty and inequality. This\nset the stage for polarization between the benefactors and losers of Mexico’s economic model. In\nthe highly polarized 2006 elections, PAN candidate Felipe Calderón won by a slim margin\n(0.56%). PRD candidate AMLO denounced the elections as fraudulent, declared himself the\nlegitimate president, and launched a movement of resistance against the incoming government.\nThis political crisis probably influenced President Calderón’s decision to deploy the army in the\ndrug war in order to gain legitimacy and demonstrate that he had the support of the army.\nSince then, the drug war has become Mexico’s primary challenge, with violence escalating to\nunprecedented levels. With the 2012 election of Enrique Peña Nieto, the PRI returned to power\nafter 12 years in opposition. After a promising first year of political accomplishments and\neconomic growth, the Tlatlaya and Ayotzinapa massacres, corruption scandals, and the\nplummeting price of oil halted the government’s reform efforts. During its final two years in office\n(2017 – 2018), the PRI implemented its policies but failed to enact any substantial changes.\nThe 2018 general elections marked a pathbreaking moment in Mexico’s recent history as a left-\nleaning party – the Movement for National Regeneration (MORENA) – won for the first time\nsince the transition to democracy in 2000. The new president, AMLO, proclaimed the “end of\nneoliberalism” and a “Fourth Transformation” that would put an end to corruption, violence and\npoverty. However, AMLO has not in fact deviated much from the previous economic model, aside\nfrom increasing state intervention in the energy sector, and has sought to concentrate power in the\npresidency.\nDuring the COVID-19 pandemic, the government declined to implement public policy to mitigate\nthe economic impact of the crisis and instead opted to impose austerity measures due to limited\nfinancial resources and an aversion to incurring debt. Although the health care system did not\nofficially collapse, hospitals faced overwhelming pressure and had to reject patients. Mexico\nexperienced one of the highest death rates in the world due to COVID-19.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 6\nThe BTI combines text analysis and numerical assessments. The score for each\nquestion is provided below its respective title. The scale ranges from 1 (worst) to\n10 (best).\nTransformation Status\nI. Political Transformation\nQuestion\n1 | Stateness\nScore\nThe state’s monopoly on the use of force continues to be challenged both by Monopoly on the\norganized drug crime and ordinary crime. AMLO has vowed to reduce the drug use of force\ncartels’ control through social policies aimed at young people, who are easy prey for 5\ncriminal groups that provide an economic livelihood. The economic consequences of\nthe pandemic made even more youth available for gangs. Since there is near-total\nimpunity in Mexico, with 97% of crimes going unpunished, the risk of being caught\nby the authorities is slight. The current government’s social policies, if successful,\nare designed for the long term.\nGuerrero, Sinaloa, Tamaulipas, Michoacán, Veracruz, Quintana Roo, Chihuahua and,\nmore recently, Jalisco are all states where drug cartels are very strong or control most\nof the state. In some cases, they have taken over state functions by distributing food\nand goods. The Cártel de Jalisco Nueva Generación (CJNG) has expanded its\npresence in Guanajuato and other areas formerly controlled by the Sinaloa cartel.\nThere are also major cartel activities and even cartel wars in Mexico City – for\nexample, between the CJNG and Unión Tepito. The resilience of civil society is\ndiminishing day by day in the most affected regions.\nThe homicide rate decreased slightly from 26 to 25.2 per 100,000 inhabitants between\n2021 and 2022; however, the absolute number of murder victims surpassed 30,000\nfor the fifth consecutive year. Just days after AMLO congratulated himself for this\nmodest decline, 25 members of the Sinaloa cartel escaped from a jail in Ciudad\nJuárez, resulting in the deaths of 10 guards and security officers in early January\n2023. The jailbreak sheds light on the extent of narcos’ control over the prison system\nand has sparked a debate on the president’s nearly nonexistent security policy.\nAnother compelling example of the drug cartels’ influence is the 2019 release of\nOvidio Guzmán López, also known as “El Chapo’s” son, when the cartels threatened\nretaliation against the population of Culiacán, even targeting areas where military\nfamilies resided. However, in early 2023, Ovidio was recaptured by the military and\nis currently awaiting extradition to the United States.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 7\nAMLO’s security strategy has consisted of almost full reliance on the military,\nexpanding its roles to an extent that may represent a future threat to democracy.\nHowever, the army is losing territorial control. U.S. pressure on the Mexican\ngovernment to combat organized crime more effectively might oblige AMLO’s\nadministration to take a more decisive approach, overcoming its laissez-faire\ncommitment that has dominated up to this point.\nMexico lacks any significant ethnic, religious, economic or political group that State identity\nadvocates for economic, social or political autonomy, let alone territorial autonomy. 8\nSince the Zapatista uprising in 1994, there has been increased public awareness of\nthe discrimination faced by Indigenous people. As a result, Indigenous movements\nhave emerged, advocating for the preservation of their identity in the face of Mexico’s\npredominant national culture, which is mestizo. However, these movements have not\nexhibited hostility toward non-Indigenous individuals or posed a challenge to the\nstate. In 1994, the constitution was amended to officially recognize Mexico as a\nmulticultural state. Additionally, in many municipalities within Oaxaca, where the\nIndigenous population is sizable, the opportunity to exercise political and civil rights\nunder Indigenous customary laws has been granted.\nThis situation has not changed under AMLO’s government. The Maya Train project\nis opposed by Indigenous communities that live in the area where it is being built,\nand the Zapatistas even declared war against the project. Still, the project is\nproceeding and has not caused significant friction with the government.\nThe separation between church and state was achieved in the mid-19th century and No interference of\nreinforced by the Mexican Revolution. Until 1992, the Mexican government did not religious dogmas\nmaintain formal relations with or officially recognize any church. The secular state 8\nfaced some challenges once the Catholic PAN ascended to the presidency and in some\nof the more Catholic states. The Catholic Church has become increasingly involved\nin issues such as abortion and same-sex marriage. States such as Guanajuato and\nQuerétaro have restricted the already very limited right to abortion, while Mexico\nCity, Oaxaca, Hidalgo, Veracruz, Baja California and Colima have passed laws that\nlegalize abortion.\nThe rise of evangelical churches and AMLO’s apparent sympathy for them also\nappears to represent a challenge to the secular state. Unlike predecessors, AMLO\nfrequently makes references to religious themes and even formed a coalition with an\nevangelical party to secure the presidency. However, this party lost its registered\nstatus and does not hold any significance in government. Nevertheless, the increasing\nsocial influence of evangelicalism in certain parts of the country, akin to what has\nhappened in Brazil, may pose a more significant challenge. In certain communities,\nevangelicals now outnumber other religious groups and are gaining prominence in\nvarious states, notably Chiapas (32.5%), Tabasco (27.1%), Campeche (24.3%), and\nQuintana Roo (20.9%). As a result, conflicts between Catholic and evangelical\ncommunities have emerged.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 8\nThe Mexican state has been able to expand its reach in areas such as education and Basic\nhealth care since the 1940s. However, as of 2020, only 57.3% of the population had administration\naccess to safely managed sanitation, and 43% had access to safely managed water 6\nsupply. These resources are particularly scarce in Indigenous regions and the suburbs\nof large cities. Approximately 10% of the population has no access to water, and in\nMexico City, over 2 million people are reliant on purchasing water from tankers.\nAdministrative capacity and public service delivery differ significantly between\nregions. The decentralization of education and health care in the 1990s has\nexacerbated this inequality. Moreover, decentralization has enormously increased the\nresources governors can manage, leading to an explosion in corruption over the past\ntwo decades. AMLO has attempted to recentralize most services. The administration\nhas centralized the health system (INSABI) by eliminating the decentralized Seguro\nPopular, thereby leaving around 10% of the population insured by Seguro Popular\nwithout any health coverage. The purchase of medicines has also been centralized,\nresulting in shortages of various medicines, especially those for children with cancer.\nThe creation of the National Guard and the corresponding abandonment of state and\nmunicipal policies has also led to the centralization of security policies.\nIn addition, the government has imposed a strict austerity program on the central\nadministration and other institutions, cutting departments and laying off officials,\nwhich has affected their activities and administrative capacity. Moreover, the\nadministration has bypassed traditional state welfare institutions by establishing an\nextended policy of direct cash transfers in various sectors. This has resulted in an\nincrease in the clientelistic functions of the state administration, thereby affecting its\nquality.\n2 | Political Participation\nSince the 1980s, general elections have become increasingly competitive, fair and Free and fair\nfree, although certain deficiencies persist. From the 1990s onward, there has been a elections\nprocess of institutionalizing political life, with the establishment of independent 7\nbodies such as the National Electoral Institute (INE) and the Electoral Tribunal of the\nFederal Judicial Branch (TEPJF), which organize and oversee elections. However,\ntheir authority is limited to official campaigns and elections. Matters that fall outside\nthe realm of campaigns and elections, as well as activities occurring outside of polling\nbooths, remain unregulated. As a result, clientelistic practices, which are practiced by\nall parties, have continued to prevail. Moreover, there exist numerous loopholes for\nthe control of “black” money derived from both legal and illegal sources.\nThere were the usual improprieties among all parties in the 2018 presidential\nelections, where AMLO, the founder of MORENA, won the presidential race with\n53% of the vote. Claims of fraud or irregularities were minimal. June’s 2021 midterm\nelections were also marred by unlawful campaign activities, including the usual vote-\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 9\nbuying, misuse of public funds and infringement of campaign finance rules. While\nMORENA and its allies, the Workers’ Party (PT) and the Green Party (PVEM),\nsustained a majority with 278 Chamber of Deputies seats, they lost their\nsupermajority in Congress, which had previously enabled them to pass constitutional\nreforms unhindered.\nAt the subnational level, the assassination of local and state political figures has\ncontinued, constituting a considerable obstacle to political activity. MORENA won\nfour out of the six gubernatorial races in 2022, securing 20 out of 32 regional\nadministrations. This outcome further highlights the president’s enduring electoral\nappeal. There are indications that drug cartels aided Morena’s triumphs. Additionally,\nMORENA candidates are likely to win the upcoming elections in the federal states\nof Mexico and Coahuila. If this proves to be the case, it will have significant\nconsequences, as it would bring an end to decades of PRI dominance in the state of\nMexico, the country’s most populous.\nAMLO has consistently criticized the INE and TEPJF, thereby undermining their\ncredibility, especially among his supporters. In December 2022, he introduced a\ncontentious electoral reform that aimed to modify the constitution and severely\nweaken the INE and the TRIFE. Although this initial proposal did not receive\ncongressional approval, AMLO proceeded to propose a “Plan B,” a less extensive\nelectoral reform package that Congress eventually endorsed in February 2023, shortly\nafter the review period concluded. Plan B would substantially reduce the INE’s\nbudget, personnel and office space, hampering the organization’s ability to oversee\nelections impartially. Legal challenges have been filed against the reform package,\nand it is currently under consideration by the Supreme Court.\nThe greatest challenge to effective power to govern is the increasingly powerful Effective power to\ncriminal groups that control some municipalities, regions and states. These groups govern\nexert pressure on elected officials and the population and even control politics 6\nthrough co-optation and violence. Traditional veto powers – such as national and\nforeign capital, as well as unions (especially the teachers’ union, the petroleum\nworkers’ union and the electrical workers’ union) – also continue to influence\npolitical life. The teachers’ union played a dominant role in a recent repeal of the\neducation reform, confirming its influence.\nThe Mexican army – under civilian control since the early 1950s – has been notably\nstrengthened since ex-President Calderón declared the war on drugs in 2006. Since\nthen, the military has been entrusted with wide-ranging tasks and granted significant\nresources. The current government has not only abandoned its promise to return the\narmy to its barracks but has also expanded its responsibilities to include the\nconstruction and administration of the new airport in Mexico City and the Maya\nTrain. The military also played a critical role in containing the COVID-19 pandemic,\nand in May 2022, a presidential decree granted the military exclusive control over\ncustoms activities. A few months later, Congress passed legislation transferring\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 10\noperative and administrative control of the National Guard – established in 2019 and\ndominated by military personnel – away from the public security ministry to the\nSecretariat of National Defense, although the constitution states that the Guard is a\ncivil institution. Legal challenges were brought against the legislation and await\ndeliberation. While the military is not a challenge to democracy right now, it may\nbecome so in the future.\nThe constitution guarantees the rights of association and assembly. However, there Association /\nare formal, informal and even illegal means by which freedom of association can be assembly rights\nundermined. These include the formation of unions before a company starts 7\nproduction, preventing workers from organizing, or forcing workers to organize in\nspecific unions that are under the control of employers. To curb such practices, the\ngovernment has amended the Federal Labor Law to allow workers to vote in secret\nto approve their collective bargaining agreements and choice of union. This change\nhas already modified the situation of some Mexican unions.\nWhile the federal government does not pressure individuals or groups to prevent them\nfrom exercising their rights, there is a lack of transparency at the state and local levels.\nThis has always been the case, but as the distinction between legal and criminal actors\nbecomes blurred in numerous states and communities, the threat posed by criminal\ngangs, up to and including murder, has risen. Social activists and journalists face the\nrisk of being targeted by criminal groups, and the high levels of impunity exacerbate\nthe dangers associated with engaging in political and social activities. Additionally,\nstate security forces, particularly the police, are notorious for engaging in arbitrary\narrests and employing excessive force.\nSince Peña Nieto’s term in government, there have been clashes between the police\nand women protesters advocating for accountability in cases of gender-based\nviolence. This trend has persisted under AMLO’s government. A noticeable rift and\na lack of effective communication have emerged between the government and civil\nsociety organizations, including environmentalists, Indigenous communities and\nvictims of violence. This divide has resulted in mobilizations and confrontations with\nthe authorities. Notably, tensions escalated between feminist activists and the\nauthorities during International Women’s Day in March 2021. During the protests,\nclashes between the police and protesters in the capital city resulted in injuries to 81\nindividuals, predominantly female police officers.\nMexico’s mass media is formally free; both de jure and mostly de facto, citizens and Freedom of\njournalists may speak and write freely without government interference. However, expression\nthe assassination of journalists by criminal organizations has persisted. Since 2006, 5\nmore than 168 journalists have been assassinated, and 28 have disappeared. A total\nof 59 of these cases have occurred during the four years of the current administration.\nApproximately 90% of these transgressions remain unpunished. In 2022, the highest\nnumber of journalist killings in a single year was recorded, and if this trend continues,\nAMLO’s presidential term will be the deadliest so far in this regard. Reporting on\ncertain topics, such as crime, drugs, and the nexus between politics and drug\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 11\ntrafficking, is highly risky or even impossible in areas controlled by cartels. Self-\ncensorship is increasingly prevalent among newspapers, particularly in regions highly\naffected by organized crime and drug trafficking. Moreover, the president openly\ncriticizes journalists who scrutinize his government during his “mañanera” press\nconferences, labeling them as conservative or corrupt. This further contributes to the\noverall climate of risk for journalists. The special rapporteur for freedom of\nexpression of the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights (IACHR) has called\nfor the suspension of the controversial program known as the “Who’s Who of Lies,”\nwhich a presidential collaborator presents every Wednesday during AMLO’s\nmañaneras.\nElectronic media ownership remains concentrated. There are two main television\nchannels – Televisa and TV Azteca – and radio is largely controlled by a few\ncompanies. Still, viewpoints on digital and print media continue to be diverse, despite\nAMLO’s attacks on critical news outlets.\n3 | Rule of Law\nThe separation of powers is constitutionally enshrined and places the Mexican Separation of\npresident in a comparatively weak position vis-à-vis Congress, unless the government powers\nhas a majority there, as is currently the case. Although MORENA and its satellite 6\nparties lost their absolute majority (which is required to change the constitution) in\nthe 2021 mid-term election, the ruling coalition can still pass laws with its simple\nmajority, making the separation between the executive and Congress virtually\nnonexistent.\nThe Supreme Court has relative independent power, although in recent years it has\naccepted all government reforms as constitutional. In principle, the court could\nbecome more resistant in the future, considering that new Court President Norma\nLucía Piña Hernández, who was elected in early 2023, has promised to remain an\nindependent figure. However, AMLO has accused the new chief justice of being\ncorrupt, along with the rest of the judiciary. It has become clear that he has no respect\nfor judicial autonomy and is actively undermining the authority of the Supreme Court.\nThe independent institutions that monitor and control the executive (e.g., the Human\nRights Institute; the National Institute for Transparency, Access to Information and\nProtection of Personal Data, INAI), have either been taken over by the governing\nparty or weakened. The same has been attempted with the electoral institutions – the\nINE and the TRIFE – through the aforementioned Plan B (see “Free and fair\nelections”). In the latest INE nominations, three out of four new commissioners had\nclose ties to MORENA.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 12\nThe Supreme Court, which also serves as a constitutional court, includes four of Independent\nAMLO’s nominees and has not yet ruled against the current government. All judiciary\nimportant decisions have favored AMLO (e.g., on the militarization of the National 5\nGuard and the Electricity Reform). While the outgoing president of the court was\nclosely aligned with AMLO, newly elected Justice Piña Hernández appears more\nindependent, suggesting that the Supreme Court’s decisions may become more\nautonomous. The president often criticizes members of the Supreme Court when he\nfears it will render decisions contrary to his political agenda.\nThe 2014 judicial reform created an independent attorney general, known in Mexican\nterminology as a “fiscal general,” thus altering the dynamics between the executive\nand judicial branches. This reform officially commenced in January 2019 during\nAMLO’s tenure. Despite this transformation, the current attorney general, Alejandro\nGertz Manero, has a checkered past involving corruption and prioritizing personal\ninterests. Moreover, he is susceptible to the influence of the president and carries out\nhis directives. Notably, the attorney general cleared General Salvador Cienfuegos, a\nformer defense secretary who faced allegations of drug-trafficking and corruption and\nwas indicted in the United States, after this figure’s return to Mexico, with the\nassurance that legal consequences would be appropriately addressed. AMLO openly\nsupported and defended this controversial decision.\nAt the state and local level, the judiciary is clearly biased toward the executive, and\ncorruption is rampant. Ordinary citizens continue to experience a highly corrupt\njudicial system in which criminals are often released or enjoy perks in prison, while\ninnocent people are detained for years before trial.\nThe AMLO government has made the fight against corruption a priority, but results Prosecution of\nso far have been sparse, if not contradictory. There are several important ongoing office abuse\ncorruption cases at the federal level that have not yet reached a verdict. Firstly, former 4\nMinister Rosario Robles, accused of heading a large illegal party financing scheme,\nspent almost four years in prison without trial and was subsequently released. In the\nOdebrecht scandal, the former head of the state oil company PEMEX, Emilio Lozoya,\nis in prison without a verdict, and negotiations are underway for him to return part of\nthe stolen money in exchange for his release. There are also several cases involving\nofficials of the current government. Most notably, Manuel Bartlett, head of the state-\nowned electricity company CFE, was accused of having a great number of properties\nthat did not correspond to his salary, but no charges were filed. Additionally, former\nDefense Minister Cienfuegos was arrested and accused of drug trafficking in the\nUnited States, but since his return to Mexico, the charges have been dismissed.\nMoreover, numerous corruption cases at lower levels have not been prosecuted,\nincluding some within the army.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 13\nCivil rights are enshrined in law, but even the most basic rights are violated in Civil rights\npractice. One of AMLO’s campaign promises was to end civil rights violations. 4\nHowever, little has changed, and civil rights continue to be violated by the police, the\narmy and even the judiciary. This has been a constant reality of life for Indigenous\npeople, the poor and sexual minorities. Migrants crossing Mexico to enter the United\nStates are also subject to all sorts of abuse by police and criminal gangs – especially\nmigrant women, who are often sexually abused.\nPrisons are filled with individuals who have committed minor offenses but lack the\nresources to secure legal representation. Consequently, they may endure years of\nimprisonment before their trials commence. A decision from the Inter-American\nCourt of Human Rights declares that Mexico’s compulsory pretrial detention system\ninfringes upon human rights. This issue has notably deteriorated in regions where\ndrug conflicts persist, and the ongoing involvement of the army’s National Guard\nvirtually ensures the perpetuation of violations against civil rights. Additionally, the\nMexican Institute of Human Rights and Democracy, which admittedly had its\nimperfections in the past, is now completely under the authority of the executive\nbranch.\nOnly a few cases that gain international attention are investigated more thoroughly,\nwith the most notable being the Ayotzinapa case of disappearances. Meanwhile,\nthousands of other victims and cases, including violence against women, are being\nignored. Femicide rates have steadily increased in recent years, from 735 reported\ncases in 2017 to 968 in 2022. Mexico City bears the highest toll, followed by the\nstates of Mexico and Veracruz. According to the United Nations, six out of 10\nMexican women have experienced violence, and an average of 10 girls and women\nare killed daily. Feminist organizations have called for the declaration of a “femicide\nemergency,” but the president has refused. Instead, he accuses the organizations of\nbeing manipulated by his political enemies.\n4 | Stability of Democratic Institutions\nAMLO’s government has concentrated power in the executive branch. Since the 2021 Performance of\nlegislative elections, the president’s MORENA party, along with its satellite parties, democratic\nhave held a simple majority in Congress. This has reduced overall friction in the institutions\nlegislature, yet MORENA’s majority simultaneously limits effective checks and 6\nbalances. The president also continues to criticize and attack the Supreme Court and\nhas launched attempts to weaken autonomous institutions that organize elections and\noversee government actions. He has limited the financial resources of electoral\noversight institutions and recently had his “Plan B” approved by Congress, which, if\nsanctioned by the Supreme Court, would weaken the INE and the TRIFE.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 14\nAdditionally, the threat to democracy from organized crime is serious, with\ncandidates and elected officials, as well as social activists, routinely assassinated. In\nsome regions controlled by drug cartels, many local elected officials and police\nagencies are under their command. In these same regions, the judiciary is also under\ntheir control. The current government has expanded the military’s prerogatives and\ntasks, thereby also giving it economic influence, which might pose a future threat to\ndemocracy.\nThe most important political and social actors in the past have been committed to the Commitment to\ncountry’s democratic institutions. Nonetheless, the present government considers democratic\nitself to be implementing a peaceful revolution, and AMLO considers himself to be institutions\nthe true representative of “the people.” This raises questions about whether power 5\nwill be peacefully transferred to the next government if MORENA loses the 2024\npresidential elections. Already, AMLO has put forward initiatives to weaken the INE\nand the TRIFE, which would undermine election oversight. The president has also\ntried to exert control over the Supreme Court, which would ultimately have the final\nsay in case of an electoral dispute. Lastly, the president may resort to the military,\nwhich he has aligned with his movement and empowered, as a “last resort” if all else\nfails.\nMajor actors, such as private firms, have adopted a strategy of informal alliance with\nthe president in order to avoid difficulties. The Catholic Church has retired from\npublic engagement, and civil society organizations (CSOs) are trying to maintain\ntheir presence through street protests. All in all, institutionalized channels of\ndemocratic participation are being bypassed by informal and extra-institutional\nprocesses.\n5 | Political and Social Integration\nIn the past, Mexico’s party system was less fragmented than those of most Latin Party system\nAmerican countries, and voter volatility was comparatively low. The PRI, PAN and 5\nPRD each usually captured around a third of the vote during elections. However, this\nchanged radically with the 2018 elections, in which MORENA received 53% of the\nvote, leaving the traditional political parties in the minority. Since then, MORENA\nhas become the new dominant party. MORENA has considerably expanded social\nprograms, which have almost all become conditional monetary transfers that can be\nused in a clientelist manner, to an extent not seen since the PRI’s one-party rule.\nAdditionally, AMLO has exacerbated polarization through discourse that depicts\nprevious governments and their sympathizers as corrupt, neoliberal, conservative and\nso on.", "excerpt_sha256": "1c5b0d973101f7d585adf2f4147c9358c8180dac332383bd84263871d6f68ab0", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The supplied excerpt describes legal challenges to Plan B and the National Guard transfer as pending; it does not contain the later invalidation decisions."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0013-E013", "source_id": "SRC-d97a061e10c2ae4b", "url": "https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf", "title": "BTI 2024 Country Report: Mexico", "publisher": "Bertelsmann Transformation Index", "raw_sha256": "6870c2da8c47fd692d47d7c7ced62ef6d31adbccee782bcf3081d8f9919b8ec1", "fact_summary": "BTI reports that AMLO expanded direct cash transfers and raised the minimum wage, but health coverage losses followed the abolition of Seguro Popular and creation of INSABI, cash programs were vulnerable to patronage, gender and Indigenous inequalities persisted, economic growth was weak, environmental policy favored fossil fuels and major projects harmed ecosystems, and R&D funding was cut.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.6", "5B.8", "6B.1", "6B.10", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.7", "6B.8", "6B.9", "8B.2", "8B.6", "8B.7", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The report covers policy and outcomes through January 2023, with indicators mainly from 2020-2022.", "ruler_attribution": "BTI attributes the reforms, transfers, energy and research choices to AMLO's government.", "limitations": ["The report predates most of 2023 and therefore cannot capture subsequent implementation or correction.", "Several welfare and environmental outcomes reflect inherited and pandemic conditions as well as AMLO-era choices."], "start_unit": 24, "end_unit": 28, "locator": "page 24 through page 28", "exact_excerpt": "BTI 2024 | Mexico 24\n10 | Welfare Regime\nThe current government has made its most significant changes in the area of social Social safety nets\nsecurity. The previously most important conditional cash transfer program, Prospera, 6\nwas canceled. However, this did not change the government’s focus on assistance\nprograms. While Prospera aid reached families through mothers, the AMLO\ngovernment now allocates funds specifically and directly to those affected: the\nelderly, women with young children, and as scholarships for children and youth in\nelementary, secondary schools and universities. All recipients must be registered by\ngovernment officials. Hence, this aid is vulnerable to patronage-style manipulation.\nMost of this aid is given to poor families and therefore is not universal. The\ngovernment has declared that 70 million people receive resources from the\ngovernment, yet some specialists have calculated that the true number is closer to 40\nmillion, similar to previous governments.\nAMLO’s cash handouts represent a new relationship with a federal government that,\nfor many decades, ignored poverty. Cash “scholarships” for attending school and\ncash payments from Lopez Obrador’s flagship Bienestar Pension for Elderly Adults\nprogram are very popular and establish a direct bond between the president and the\npeople. However, with their direct short-term transfers, these programs fail to support\nthe development of skills that would have medium- to long-term effects for\nindividuals. It remains unclear where most of the cash transfers end up. One of the\nmost expensive programs, the $20 billion subsidy for gasoline, is used by AMLO to\nmaintain popular support.\nThe pension system has never covered more than 30% of the population. AMLO has\nnot reversed the reform from a pay-as-you-go to an individual capitalization system.\nMany people facing unemployment during the pandemic withdrew a portion of their\nsavings from pension funds, which decreased the system’s efficiency further.\nThe public health care system is divided into three systems: one for civil servants,\nanother for private sector workers and a third for the remaining population. The\nadministration ended the Seguro Popular program, which was introduced in 2005 as\nthe third pillar. It introduced a universal health care system, INSABI, without\nsignificantly increasing the budget. The creation of an entirely new system with little\nplanning on the eve of the pandemic contributed to very poor management of the\nhealth crisis. INEGI surveys show an increase in the share of people without health\ncoverage – 28.2% in 2020 vs. 16.2% in 2018.\nThe health system is underfunded compared to other countries in Latin America and\nthe Caribbean region. Public expenditure on health was only 3.3% of GDP in 2020,\nalbeit up 0.6% from 2019. This results in extremely high out-of-pocket expenditures\n(about 45%) compared to countries like Brazil and Argentina (30%). Life expectancy\nis around the regional median, averaging 75.1 years, which is the same as in 2019.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 25\nDiscrimination against the Indigenous population and gender inequality are structural Equal opportunity\nissues in Mexico, resulting in slow change at best. The administration has made no 5\nreal effort to address discrimination or promote gender equality. The Indigenous\npopulation, which accounts for 6% to 10% of the total population, continues to\nexperience marginalization. Although Mexico’s overall literacy rate in 2020 was\n95.2%, it was only 67% among Indigenous groups. In 2015, only 72.6% of\nIndigenous people completed primary school, with 16.6% having no formal\neducation. In contrast, among the general population, the figures were 81.7% and 5%,\nrespectively. Despite these disparities, student enrollment has increased overall. In\n2021, the primary school enrollment rate reached 104.7%, while enrollment rates in\nsecondary and tertiary education stood at 104.6% and 142.6%, respectively.\nHowever, progress in terms of student learning outcomes, high school completion\nrates and college enrollment has been modest. Although the achievement gap\nbetween Indigenous and non-Indigenous students has decreased since 2006, it\nremains significant.\nLiteracy and school attendance rates among women are nearly equal to those among\nmen at all levels. In 2020, the literacy rate among women was 94.5%, compared to\n96.1% for men. Additionally, enrollment rates were equal between sexes at the\nprimary school level and slightly higher for women at the secondary and tertiary\neducation levels, according to the World Bank. Nevertheless, significant disparities\npersist in the labor market. The employment rate for women in 2021 was quite low,\nat 38.5%, comparable to levels seen in poorer countries in the Northern Triangle and\nfar behind Brazil, Costa Rica, Uruguay, Chile, Argentina and Colombia. However,\nthis figure does not account for women working in the informal sector. This\nexacerbates, rather than alleviates, gender inequality because working conditions and\nsalaries in the informal sector are much lower than in the formal sector, despite equal\nnumbers of men and women working in this sector. In terms of wage equality for\nsimilar work, Mexico has the worst score on the continent at 0.51 – meaning that\nwomen are 49% less likely than men to receive an equal wage for similar work –\nwhile Costa Rica scores 0.65 and Uruguay 0.61 on this issue. Inequality for women\nis also evident in the high rate of femicide and the prevalent “machista” culture.\n11 | Economic Performance\nIn economic terms, the past four years have been some of the worst in recent decades Output strength\nfor Mexico. This is primarily attributed to several factors: the cancellation of the 5\nMexico City Airport project, which greatly affected numerous investing enterprises\nand providers; the tremendous impact of the pandemic on GDP (a decline of 9.1%);\nthe absence of an active countercyclical policy to mitigate the pandemic’s economic\neffects; and, finally, the president’s polarizing discourse, which has created\nuncertainty for domestic capital. This uncertainty has been further exacerbated by the\nflow of foreign capital, which is driven by geopolitical tensions between major\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 26\ncountries, particularly the United States and China. In this context, above all, Western\ncountries view Mexico as a good location for their manufacturing plants, with the\nfree trade agreements in place adding to this appeal.\nFDI in Mexico increased by 12% in 2022 to just under $35.3 billion, up from $31.54\nbillion in 2021. Forty-eight percent of FDI was new, while 45% came from\nreinvestment of profits. Manufacturers of vehicles, electronic components and auto\nparts were among the biggest recipients of foreign investment, with most funds\ncoming from the United States ($15 billion). The “nearshoring” phenomenon – the\nrelocation of companies to Mexico to be close to the U.S. market – benefited the\nMexican economy in 2022 with significant investment flowing into northern states\nand the central Bajío region.\nIn 2021, GDP per capita barely rebounded to nearly the 2016 level at $19,578\naccording to the World Bank. This recovery was barely evident in the unemployment\ndata due to the absence of unemployment insurance, as well as the significant levels\nof underemployment and informality. Although the level of gross capital investment\nhas remained low, it is not significantly lower than in previous years. However, if the\ncountry is to escape the middle-income trap, investment must increase significantly.\nInflation has been on the rise since 2021, mirroring global trends, with an initial rate\nof 5.7% that climbed to a two-decade high of 7.2% in 2022. As of December 2022,\nthe national debt totaled 51.7% of the country’s nominal GDP, representing a\nconsistent trend over recent years.\nIn contrast to the government’s economic performance, its macroeconomic policies\nhave remained highly orthodox, both in terms of public debt and deficit. The\nInternational Monetary Fund (IMF) even suggested that the government should have\nrelaxed its austerity measures during the pandemic in order to alleviate the\npopulation’s suffering and lay the groundwork for a more robust economic recovery\nafterward. However, the significant current account deficit of $25 billion in the first\nnine months of 2022 is concerning.\n12 | Sustainability\nAMLO’s government has banned fracking (although there are unconfirmed reports Environmental\nthat some fracking zones are still active), but all other policies fail to take ecology policy\ninto account, and the Ministry of Ecology’s budget has been significantly cut. The 3\nadministration’s most important economic policy seeks to recover the state’s\nhegemony in the oil and electricity sectors. In attempting to do so, it has increased\nenergy production using carbon resources and fuel oil (mostly in private foreign\nhands), discouraging investment in alternative energy sources. The government has\ndirected resources toward the fossil fuels sector, insisting on using oil and gas from\nnational production and categorizing renewable energy projects as damaging to the\nnational economy. The 2013 energy reform that liberalized the electricity sector,\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 27\ncreating room for new players, clean energy and foreign investment, has been\nreversed. The implementation of the Energy Reform Mexico is forecast to lead to an\nincrease of over 46% in greenhouse gas emissions. Rating agencies have warned\nabout the possibility of lowering Mexico’s rating in 2022 due to the magnitude of\ngovernment support received by Petróleos Mexicanos (PEMEX) and the Federal\nElectricity Commission (CFE), which pose a risk to public finances. Additionally,\nthe government is constructing a refinery on the coast of Tabasco in a mangrove area,\nas well as the Maya Train system; the latter will have a significant impact on the\necology of the Mayan Peninsula through the construction of train tracks, train stations\nand the development of a destructive commercial tourist industry around it.\nPublic awareness of environmental problems has grown in recent decades, largely\ndue to the activities of environmental NGOs. AMLO has criticized these\norganizations for their critique of government ventures. According to the NGO\nGlobal Witness, Mexico has become the most dangerous place in the world for\nactivists defending the environment and land. In 2021 alone, 54 activists were\nreported killed. The Yaquis, an Indigenous community in the northern part of the\ncountry, are also embroiled in various conflicts – from Mexican drug cartels to\nmining operations pursuing their land for lithium extraction.\nDespite President AMLO’s original commitment to banning fracking, the budget\nallocated toward fracking reportedly increased in 2022. Residents of the affected\ncommunities in Veracruz have protested and complained about the environmental\nimpacts, which include air pollution, frequent fires caused by oil spills, contamination\nof aquifers, and the loss of biodiversity and natural landscapes. The government has\nneglected to engage with environmental organizations and has not genuinely\nconsulted Indigenous communities.\nEven before the pandemic, PISA tests ranked Mexico in the bottom third of tested Education policy /\ncountries in the areas of science, mathematics and reading – well below the OECD R&D\naverage and only slightly above the Latin American average. The pandemic may have 4\na tremendous impact on the educational level of small children and adolescents,\nconsidering that Mexico closed its schools longer than almost all other countries\nworldwide – 250 days. The extent of the impact cannot yet be assessed since the PISA\ntests have not been applied in the last four years. Furthermore, spending on education\nhas been decreasing; in 2022, it hit the lowest level in a decade, at 3.1% of GDP.\nThe education problem is not only financial but also political. The elementary school\nsystem is dominated by the teachers’ union – the largest union in Mexico and a\nremnant of the former authoritarian regime. The current government’s main focus has\nbeen giving scholarships to students from the primary school through university\nlevels, although not in a universal manner, except at the secondary school level.\n\nBTI 2024 | Mexico 28\nWith a spending of only 0.43% of GDP on research and development (R&D), Mexico\nclearly lags behind countries such as Brazil, which spends 1.2% of its GDP in this\narea. This is the lowest level of R&D investment among OECD countries, behind\nTürkiye, Poland and Slovakia, and lower than many other countries with similar\nlevels of development. R&D is not a priority for the current government, considering\nthat it cut the budget of the National Council of Science and Technology\n(CONACYT) research consortium by half. Additionally, scholarships for Mexican\npostgraduates wanting to study abroad were significantly cut back, favoring the\npursuit of domestic degrees. The case of CIDE, an internationally recognized higher\neducation institution that has been continuously targeted by the government, has\nillustrated the current precarity of universities’ autonomy The most\n                    pronounced downgrade came in the category of civil society and media,\n          as Mexican journalists continue to face adverse working conditions, including\n          the world’s highest rate of violence against reporters outside Ukraine. The country’s institutional short-\n          comings against corruption continue to hamper the resolution of corruption cases.\n\n\n          CRITICAL ISSUES TO MONITOR\n\n          Despite an increase in the budget for anti-corruption institutions under the National\n          Anti-corruption System (SNA) for 2023, the program continues to face implementation\n          challenges. IP address: 46.121.210.146, on 31 Jul 2026 at 14:41:55, subject to the Cambridge Core terms of\nuse, available at https://www.cambridge.org/core/terms. https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009460286.011\n\n362                               fr an cis ca pou gime\u0002nez\n\n                       Overall, AMLO’s challenges to the rule of law are numerous, as the\n                    following two sections illustrate. The president has repeatedly expressed\n                    a deep distaste for all things legal. He has portrayed the law as an\n                    instrument of the ancien régime and legal requirements as hurdles in\n                    the way of attaining the “Fourth Transformation of Mexico.”58 A painful,\n                    paradigmatic illustration of this approach to the law is the executive\n                    decree that declared public works of infrastructure (such as the construc-\n                    tion of the Maya Train) to be “national security” issues, obliging all\n                    administrative units to issue the corresponding permits notwithstanding\n                    any statutory norm to the contrary.59\n                       An abundance of developments reveal tensions along the ﬁve dimen-\n                    sions that Shaffer and Sandholtz identify as deﬁning a goal-oriented\n                    understanding of the rule of law. To illustrate some of them, this chapter\n                    focuses on two clusters of developments. The ﬁrst is the frontal attack on\n                    the National Electoral Institute, the independent agency that remains the\n                    key guarantor of electoral regularity. The second is the militarization of\n                    state functions – ﬁrst, public security, and later a much wider set of state\n                    responsibilities, including the provisions of public services and the con-\n                    struction of public works.\n                       All of them reveal a willingness to eliminate checks and balances;\n                    a general disregard for the idea of reason-giving; a decline in the predict-\n                    ability of public action since many measures are processed speedily (with\n                    or without the intervention of a dominated legislature), often in open\n                    violation of legal requirements. As in other situations of autocratic\n                    legalism, the pattern is gradual, with the overall impact being greater\n                    than the sum of its parts. Intertwined with that pattern, however, is\n                    degree of legal contestation that has no precedent in Mexico. However, under the political system\n               progressively installed by the PRI, elections were rigged and won by the\n               person designated by the president. The PRI was to a certain extent\n               inclusive of various social groups and provided a system of elite\n               rotation.61 It also provided peace and stability in a country that had\n               endured signiﬁcant turmoil during the nineteenth century.62 But it was\n               not a democracy: there were formal elections, but no actual rule-of-law\n               preconditions to ensure free voting and political competition.\n                  When it ultimately became impossible to maintain the system without\n               changes, several constitutional reforms introduced electoral competition\n               and progressively built independent electoral authorities.63 An institu-\n               tion called IFE (Instituto Federal Electoral) was created in 1990, together\n               with parallel institutions at state level. In 1996, the IFE gained complete\n               independence from the executive, and in 2013 a constitutional reform\n               renamed the institution INE (Instituto Nacional Electoral) and gave it\n               more powers over the organization and monitoring of elections at state\n               level. The institution soon established itself as one marked by profession-\n               alization (its employees are career ofﬁcials) and a model of election\n               organization and supervision that included extensive input from the\n               citizenry and was grounded in administrative rule of law. Thus, the\n               voting booths in Mexico are managed by citizens who are selected on\n               the basis of their birth month, trained before the elections by the INE, and\n               60\n                  Tom Ginsburg deﬁnes the rule of law in the administration of elections as a key compo-\n                  nent of democracy. The INE General Council’s members do not\n                    need to be lawyers.\n                       The INE is a huge institution responsible for many tasks: it issues voter\n                    ID; it organizes the elections and trains the citizens who run the voting\n                    and do the counting; it reviews whether candidates satisfy the conditions\n                    of eligibility and monitors observance of legal requirements concerning\n                    access to and use of funding and access to mass media space and time\n                    during campaigns and precampaigns; and it supervises certain aspects of\n                    political party life outside electoral processes.\n                       Despite winning 53.19 percent of the vote in an election that, by all\n                    accounts, was impeccable, AMLO soon leveled ﬁerce criticism at the\n                    INE – initially, as part of a broader campaign against all independent\n                    agencies as well as the judiciary. The attacks on INE, however, continued\n                    to grow and, over time, reached astounding levels. (The 2006 election had\n                    been narrowly won by Felipe Calderón, and from that moment AMLO\n                    became disillusioned with the INE, despite the great credibility of the\n                    system and the political alternation in the presidency.) One of the more\n                    worrying episodes occurred in April 2021, when the INE, applying\n                    existing law, cancelled the candidacy of Félix Salgado Macedonio in the\n                    Guerrero gubernatorial election because he failed to report on his pre-\n                    campaign expenditures (paid in Mexico with public money).64 Salgado\n                    then mounted a demonstration in front of the INE, which included the\n                    presentation of a huge cofﬁn and the issuing of assassination threats\n                    against the INE president; threats were also made against INE counsel-\n                    lors, whose home addresses Salgado urged his followers to locate. Salgado\n                    (who had also been denounced for several instances of sexual abuse) was\n                    the candidate AMLO wanted to win in Guerrero, and the president did\n                    not abandon him. He rather defended Salgado, saying that the INE\n                    should have imposed a ﬁne but not prevented him from running.65 In\n                    the end, MORENA selected the daughter of Salgado Macedonio as the\n                    candidate – and she won the election. IP address: 46.121.210.146, on 31 Jul 2026 at 14:41:55, subject to the Cambridge Core terms of\nuse, available at https://www.cambridge.org/core/terms. https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009460286.011\n\n10 rule-of-law crisis                                                           367\n\n               support by negotiating with the other political parties, which felt the\n               pressure of the popular march.74 A few days later, however, on\n               December 13, 2022, with the holiday season about to start, the president\n               made a surprising move and submitted to Congress the so-called Plan\n               B electoral reform. This contained a proposal to amend six statutes\n               (something MORENA could do by a simple majority vote). At its core,\n               it involved a radical shrinking and weakening of INE as an institution, to\n               the point of compromising its ability to organize and supervise elections\n               under the rule of law.75\n                   Several parts of the project are perfectly acceptable, even to be wel-\n               comed, such as the overdue guarantee of the right for people in prison to\n               vote, the regulation of online trials, the simpliﬁcation of complaint\n               procedures, the need to implement gender parity in elections for state\n               governors, and the restructuring of the calendar for federal and state\n               elections. Yet other proposals are sensitive, and some deeply troubling. In\n               this last category are the elimination of the rule that had so far prevented\n               individuals holding public ofﬁce (and in receipt of a salary paid with\n               public money) from engaging in political advocacy in electoral cam-\n               paigns; the elimination of the INE administrative apparatus across the\n               country, endangering electoral IDs and the training of citizens who serve\n               on election day; the reorganization of the INE’s internal units, with the\n               immediate removal of the executive secretary, the dissolution of the\n               trusts guaranteeing the salaries of INE employees (which would now\n               depend on annual budgetary allocations); and the provision (tailored to\n               “save” a future Salgado Macedonio) that being sanctioned by the INE for\n               violating electoral law is no impediment to run for elections (only\n               a criminal conviction is).\n                   Plan B was published in two steps. IP address: 46.121.210.146, on 31 Jul 2026 at 14:41:55, subject to the Cambridge Core terms of\nuse, available at https://www.cambridge.org/core/terms. https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009460286.011\n\n368                               fr an cis ca pou gime\u0002nez\n\n                    were curtailed, and by actors, who argued that the reform affected electoral\n                    processes already underway, thus disregarding the prohibition on making\n                    amendments to electoral rules in the ninety days preceding an election.76\n                    Before the publication of the remaining package of reforms, civil society\n                    organizations summoned a new march in defense of the vote on\n                    February 26, 2023, with great success. This time, people gathered at the\n                    Zócalo, the tectonic power center in Mexico, and available estimates put\n                    the attendance in Mexico City and other cities across the country at\n                    approximately 700,000.77\n                       On March 4, 2023, however, the second package of Plan B was published.\n                    A few days later, the INE ﬁled two controversias against the reform and two\n                    groups of Congress members ﬁled acciones de inconstitucionalidad.78 As the\n                    demonstrators in the February march made clear through their banners and\n                    chants, protection of democracy and the rule of law would depend on the\n                    Supreme Court.\n                       AMLO has striven to undermine the Court using all means. He has\n                    taken advantage of the opportunity to appoint four justices, two of whom\n                    are unconditional supporters, while the other two have proven to be fairly\n                    independent. Yet, for a statute to be declared void in abstract review in\n                    Mexico, and disappear from the books, there must be a qualiﬁed majority\n                    of eight votes – a threshold difﬁcult to pass in any scenario, but specially\n                    so in the current climate. Further, for four years the presidency of the\n                    Court was held by Arturo Zaldívar, who was quickly criticized for\n                    cultivating an excessive closeness to the government. Under his presi-\n                    dency, the Court delayed decisions in many politically sensitive cases.\n                       In the electoral domain, however, the Court’s decision-making is\n                    subject to time limits. And its performance as regards Plan B has been\n                    admirably solid, even in the face of all imaginable kinds of pressure. It was in this subor-\n               dinated quality that the army was involved in the dark episodes of the\n               “dirty war” of the 1970s.83 Yet, during the gradual transition to democ-\n               racy of the 1980s and 1990s, the military was generally held in high regard\n               by the population – which would associate it with assistance during\n               natural disasters, among other tasks of that sort.\n                 The situation changed when Felipe Calderón won the presidency in\n               2006. Some two years later, although the matter had been mentioned\n               during his campaign, Calderón decided to launch a “war against drugs”\n               that put the army on the streets in several parts of the country without\n               any legal basis for doing so. In December 2017, during the presidency of\n               Enrique Peña Nieto, and partly responding to demands from the army\n\n                  y operará con normatividad anterior [INE suspends the 2023 electoral reform and will\n                  operate under the previous regulation], C E N T R A L E L E C T O R A L (Mar. 28, 2023), https://\n                  tinyurl.com/yc2cy963.\n               80\n                  Beatriz Guillén, Morena propone modiﬁcar un artículo para que los jueces sean elegidos por\n                  voto popular [Morena proposes modiﬁcation of an article to have all judges elected by\n                  popular vote], E L P A Í S (Mar. 24, 2023), https://tinyurl.com/59z7hxsn.\n               81\n                  Zewdryk Raziel, El Supremo invalida la primera parte del Plan B de López Obrador\n                  [Supreme invalidates ﬁrst part of López Obrador’s Plan B], E L P A Í S (May 8, 2023),\n                  https://tinyurl.com/4dwtxbvt.\n               82\n                  José Ramón Cossío, Drama constitucional en tres actos: ¿Por qué culpar a otros de la propia\n                  arrogancia o impericia? the statute wrongfully allowed\n                    for army participation in ordinary daily tasks of public security, which\n                    had to be fulﬁlled by ordinary political forces.86\n                       By the time AMLO became president, the degree of damage caused in\n                    the country by the “war against drugs” was already incalculable.87\n                    Everybody expected a change of strategy from López Obrador, who\n                    during the campaign had advocated for the massive problems of violence\n                    to be managed “with hugs, not bullets” (abrazos, no balazos). Soon after\n                    taking ofﬁce, however, in March 2019, with the cooperation of the\n                    inoperative “opposition” parties, he submitted an amendment to\n                    Article 21 of the Constitution to create the National Guard.88 Congress\n                    approved fast-tracking this reform and four complementary statutes: the\n                    Law of the National Guard, the Law on the National Registry of\n                    Detentions, the National Law on the Use of Force, and the General Law\n                    of the Public Security System,89 all of them raising frontal challenges to\n                    controls over the arbitrary exercise of power under the rule of law.90\n                    84\n                       Jaime Cárdenas Gracia, Ley de seguridad interior y militarización [Homeland security law\n                       and militarization] 40 C U E S T I O N E S C O N S T I T U C I O N A L E S 277 (2019).\n                    85\n                       SCJN, Nov. 15, 2018, Acciones de inconstitucionalidad 6/2018, 8/2018, 9/2018, 10/2018,\n                       11/2018.\n                    86\n                       Id. §§ 117–61.\n                    87\n                       Francisca Pou Giménez & Catalina Pérez Correa, Prohibitionist Drug Policy in Mexico:\n                       A Systemic Constitutional Underminer, 31 W A S H . These\n               were the exact words that the IACtHR had used when condemning\n               Mexico in its Alvarado Espinoza ruling93 concerning a woman whom\n               the army forcibly abducted while deploying tasks of ordinary security.\n               The IACtHR declared that the army’s involvement in these tasks was\n               inadmissible; only in an extraordinary, regulated, supervised, subordin-\n               ated, and complementary manner could the army be involved in them.94\n                  The statutes relating to the National Guard were deeply troubling and\n               were soon challenged before the Supreme Court by the national ombuds-\n               person (still directed by a person appointed during the former\n               administration).95 The Law on the National Guard, for instance, deﬁnes\n               the functions of the National Guard in a most open-ended way, paving\n               the way to clearly unconstitutional action. It does not\n                    indicate what action is to be taken when events occur that put the\n                    detentions database at risk, and it is ambiguous regarding the obligations\n                    of the army when participating in tasks of public security, since it can be\n                    interpreted to mean that the army is not subject to the constitutional\n                    obligation to immediately communicate any detention to the police and\n                    provide the information necessary for it to be properly registered, and\n                    thereby allow oversight of rule-of-law protections.97\n                       The National Law on the Use of Force similarly fails to regulate the\n                    minimum elements required by the constitutional amendment that\n                    created the National Guard and fails to specify the scope of crucial\n                    notions such as fuerza epiletal. and means of force, including ﬁrearms\n                    and deadly weapons, in “illicit” demonstrations – a notion that is left\n                    undeﬁned – or demonstrations that become violent.98 The General Law\n                    of the Public Security System, for its part, violates the right of access to\n                    public information by authorizing total ex ante On November 29, 2022, the Court declared the\n               “Militarist Decree” valid on the basis of the purely formal argument that it\n               did not contradict the literal text of the Constitution,116 ignoring IACtHR\n               decisions. Yet on April 20, 2023, on the basis of a draft prepared by one of\n               AMLO’s appointees, the Supreme Court declared the transfer of the\n               National Guard to the military branch invalid and ordered civilian control\n               of this body from January 1, 2024.117 Given the amount of political pressure\n               the judges had endured – including insertions in national newspapers paid\n               by MORENA governors requiring the Court to certify the validity of the\n               reform – this was seen as an immense victory by the law’s opponents.\n                  The Court also discussed the question of automatic pretrial prevent-\n               ive imprisonment, but there was no consensus over declaring it invalid.\n               The fact that it features in the Constitution, and not just in statutes,\n               posed a great problem for a Court that has never declared the uncon-\n               stitutionality of a constitutional amendment. The AMLO administration in\n                    Mexico viewed basic rule-of-law requirements as daily obstacles to the\n                    fulﬁllment of its political program. In contrast to other autocratic\n                    legalists, who have tried to conceal contortions of the law or bypass,\n                    rather than openly disregard, constitutional requirements, President\n                    López Obrador has not hidden his distaste for legal “barriers.” He has\n                    deﬁed them, among other reasons because he knows that the law is\n                    something people can be polarized about in countries where compli-\n                    ance with the law has not historically been an unconditional good, and\n                    where constitutional democracy has not brought about the material\n                    progress expected.120\n\n                    118\n                        Tzompaxtle Tecpile et al. v.
