It also identified climate and disaster exposure as disproportionately affecting poor and marginalized populations.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.8", "6B.1", "6B.10", "6B.3", "6B.6", "8B.1"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The document was prepared in 2022 and provides baseline and policy context for assessing 2023, not a 2023 outcome measurement.", "ruler_attribution": "The stated priorities are those of the López Obrador government, while the poverty and exposure data describe inherited and ongoing structural conditions.", "limitations": ["Most indicators cited predate 2023 and cannot establish end-of-period outcomes."], "start_unit": 9, "end_unit": 11, "locator": "page 9 through page 11", "exact_excerpt": "The World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nIBRD PROGRAM DOCUMENT FOR A PROPOSED LOAN TO THE UNITED MEXICAN STATES\n1. INTRODUCTION AND COUNTRY CONTEXT\n1. The proposed Development Policy Loan (DPL) operation supports the Government of Mexico\n(GoM) program to foster inclusive and sustainable economic growth. It aims to support components of\nthe authorities’ policy measures to: (a) support vulnerable groups and regions to reap the benefits of\neconomic recovery; and (b) reform key policies and institutions that enable sustainable economic\ngrowth. It will accomplish this by focusing on reforms that continue simplifying the business environment\nfor micro, small, and medium enterprises (MSMEs); fostering financial inclusion; and strengthening\ninstitutions and capacity for sustainable management and conservation of forest and disaster risk\nmanagement. This approach provides flexibility in a highly uncertain environment while ensuring\nadequate focus on supporting the vulnerable and addressing institutional bottlenecks while maintaining\nfiscal and environmental sustainability.\n2. Mexico is an upper middle-income country and a member of the OECD, but its development\nmodel fails to deliver in terms of economic growth, inclusion, and poverty reduction compared to its\npeers (Box 1). In fact, Mexico’s development level masks significant divisions across income, rural,\ngender, ethnicity, or geographical aspects, among others. The national multidimensional poverty rate\nreported by CONEVAL was 43.9 percent of the population (55.7 million people) in 2020. On average,\nfemale-headed households have an income of MXN 4,500 compared to MXN 4,520 for male-headed\nhouseholds. Rural households have an income of MXN 2,935 compared to MXN 4,989 for urban\nhouseholds (World Bank estimates based on 2020 ENIGH). Institutional weaknesses, whether at the\nfederal or subnational level, hamper access to public services or financial services, which in turn further\nlimit job opportunities. Rural areas particularly suffer from a vicious cycle of low productivity, low\ninvestments in physical and human capital, and high poverty rates, especially in the south of the country.3\n3. GoM priorities of addressing regional and income inequality4 over the last two years have been\nseverely undermined by the ongoing COVID pandemic. Due to the pandemic, economic growth in 2020\nwas −8.2 percent, the largest output collapse in Mexico since the 1930s. Gross domestic product (GDP)\ngrowth in Mexico was 4.8 percent in 2021 driven by strong US growth and recovery of services, the\nreopening of the economy and a steady vaccination pace. According to INEGI’s monthly employment\nsurvey - ENOE, by July 2020, 6.5 million jobs had been lost due to the pandemic, but by February 2022,\nthe economic recovery had managed to create an additional 300,000 jobs beyond pre-pandemic levels.\nStill, employment remains precarious, and more than 30 million workers (54.6 percent of total\nemployment) were informally employed in February 2022, a slight decrease compared to pre-pandemic\nlevels. Unemployment and underemployment remain above pre-pandemic levels, and a higher share of\nworkers earn less than the minimum wage. Women have been especially hard hit by the pandemic, and\nfemale labor force participation is yet to recover from its already low pre-pandemic level.5 The\nemployment recovery also remains uneven across states, with regions like Sinaloa, Tabasco, Veracruz,\nand Mexico City still below pre-pandemic employment levels. More than half of the households in rural\n3 Southern states such as Oaxaca, Guerrero, and Chiapas have multidimensional poverty rates that are above 60 percent.\n4 National Development Plan (NDP) 2019–2024. https://lopezobrador.org.mx/wp-content/uploads/2019/05/PLAN-NACIONAL-\nDE-DESARROLLO-2019-2024.pdf.\n5 At 43.5. percent female labor force participation continues to be well below the OECD and regional averages.\nPage 5\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nareas still report lower income than before the pandemic, and 19 percent of rural households report\nfood insecurity, compared to 10 percent before the pandemic (World Bank High Frequency Survey,\n2021). Remittances from migrants abroad have been especially useful during the pandemic, as\nremittance income represented 6.5 percent of incomes in the poorest decile in 2020 (World Bank\nestimate based on 2020 ENIGH).\n4. This DPL fosters institutional strengthening; and supports both the provision of global public\ngoods and Mexico’s climate change agenda:\n• Persistent institutional constraints hamper Mexico’s economic development. Institutional\nweaknesses hamper financial sector competition and limit access to finance to most productive\nfirms as well as households’ financial inclusion. As a result, most productive firms cannot grow\nand households, particularly headed by women, cannot build productive assets. Moreover,\ninstitutional constraints to formalization, such as an overly complex business environment force\nmany firms to remain in the informal sector, further hampering chances to increase their\nproductivity. Low revenue mobilization is partially explained by tax expenditures or low\nsubnational receipts. This affects the fiscal space available to expand access to and quality of\npublic services and infrastructure development. It also provides insufficient resources to close the\nincome gaps across Mexico’s regions. Reforms supported by this operation will strengthen\ninstitutions to cope with some of these challenges. Reforms supported will provide Mexico’s\nInternal Revenue Service with new monitoring tools to combat tax evasion and avoidance. Also,\nthe adoption of secondary regulations to the 2018 Fintech Law will enhance regulatory certainty\nand strengthen institutions on regulatory governance, quality, and enforcement. Furthermore,\nthe reforms supported by this operation will strengthen the Ministry of Environment’s\n(SEMARNAT) capacity to protect ecosystems and, in collaboration with the National Institute of\nthe Indigenous Peoples (INPI), preserve the rights of Indigenous Peoples (IPs) to land and natural\nresources.\n• Global public goods are supported around forest management and conservation, and the\nconservation of carbon stocks in line with Mexico’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC).\nThe operation also contributes to climate change adaptation and mitigation challenges, as forest\nconservation is key in achieving the NDC’s mitigation and adaptation targets. Forest sector\nremovals already represent 22 percent of Mexico’s total emissions and Mexico has established a\nzero net deforestation target. Better preserved forests and enhanced conservation areas will\nsupport the country and local population in enhancing adaptive capacities. This is particularly\nrelevant given Mexico’s high vulnerability to natural hazards and exposure to climate change\nimpacts,6 mainly earthquakes and climate-induced hydro-meteorological events. The country lies\nover five tectonic plates7, two of which cross the San Andreas Fault, making it one of the most\nseismologically active regions in the world8. Hydro-meteorological events such as floods, storms,\n6 Mexico ranks among the top 30 countries worldwide most exposed to disasters from natural hazards, experiencing an increase\nin the intensity of droughts, rain, and tropical cyclones and stronger extreme weather events from El Niño. University of Notre\nDame. 2015. Global Adaptation Index Country Index Technical Report.\nhttps://gain.nd.edu/assets/254377/nd_gain_technical_document_2015.pdf.\n7 Servicio Geológico Mexicano, (2017). Evolución de la tectónica en México. Gobierno de México.\nhttps://www.sgm.gob.mx/Web/MuseoVirtual/Riesgos-geologicos/Evolucion-tectonica-Mexico.html\n8 https://climateknowledgeportal.worldbank.org/country/mexico.\nPage 6\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nand landslides happen more frequently than earthquakes and are likely to be exacerbated by\nclimate change. Also, the Mexican coasts are exposed to hurricanes and cyclones during summers,\nand to “El Niño” weather events regularly, and their frequency and severity are also likely to\nincrease due to climate change. The potential rise in sea level would directly affect coastal and\nmarine ecosystems. This climate-driven increase in vulnerability could destroy public and private\ninfrastructure,9 jeopardize food systems, deepen inequality disproportionally affecting the most\nmarginalized and vulnerable populations10, and eroding efforts to reduce poverty, as around 60\npercent of the Mexican population affected by natural disasters are poor.11 Mexico is ranked as\nthe 82nd least vulnerable country to climate change but also the 83rd least ready country12,\nshowing the country’s inadequate response capacity, particularly in good governance and\ninstitutional strengthening, promotion of education, and social innovation.\n5. The proposed operation follows the Green, Resilient, and Inclusive Development (GRID) and\npromotes economic growth with environmental goals (forest protection), equitable (rural development)\nand social inclusion (access to financial services), and sustainable management of forest protection that\nwill be a source of equitable economic growth, helping to close the rural/urban income gap, by creating\njobs around sustainable natural resource management. Strengthened institutions in the environment\nsector will protect natural resources that are a key component of Mexico’s sustainable growth model,\nand the source of income and jobs, mostly for poor rural populations, many of them vulnerable and a\nlarge share of which are IPs. Institutions to deliver assistance in case of disaster would mitigate economic\nlosses and protect vulnerable populations against income losses. Also, the adoption of a simplified\nregime for MSMEs and self-employed individuals reduces administrative costs while strengthening the\ntax authority's capacity to combat tax evasion and avoidance, a critical structural challenge in Mexico.\nThe operation supports policies and institutional reforms to facilitate financial inclusion, with a focus on\nMexican migrants in the United States, facilitating remittances and access to finance to build productive\nassets for economic growth. It also regulates the market for digital payment systems to further increase\nfinancial inclusion and increase financial sector competition to overcome constraints to productivity\ngrowth. It will also create more resilient payment systems that can be instrumental during natural\ndisasters, mainly in rural areas. Policy reforms under pillar 2 will specifically benefit vulnerable\npopulations and regions in rural areas in Mexico and link the provision of global public goods to local co-\nbenefits through enhanced adaptative capacity and better preservation of local income opportunities\nbased on natural resource management. This is aligned with the GRID approach that seeks to foster\nsolutions that do not leave anyone behind and reduce disparities in opportunities and policy outcomes.\n9 According to annual damage reports from CENAPRED compiled by the World Bank, during the period 2000 – 2019, disaster\nrelated damages in public and private infrastructure totaled 733 billion pesos. Mexico is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of\nglobal climate change, including sea-level rise affecting coastal areas and inland basins, an increase in the sea surface temperature\nin the Gulf of Mexico, intensified hurricanes, increasingly heavy rains and strong storms, longer and more frequent droughts, and\nnet decreases in water run-off. CENAPRED annual \"Impacto Socioeconómico de los Principales Desastres Ocurridos en México\",\ncompiled by the World Bank. Original reports are available:\nhttp://www.cenapred.unam.mx/PublicacionesWebGobMX/buscar_buscaSubcategoria.\n10 Climate change impacts and natural disasters disproportionately affect women, indigenous peoples (IPs), Afro-descendants,\nand people with disability in Mexico, who often experience higher rates of mortality, morbidity, and diminished earnings. Climate\nchange also disproportionally affects most vulnerable Mexican populations who are more prone to health risks and often have\nless access to health services and preventive measures. The authorities' countercyclical fiscal response remains cautious with public sector borrowing\nrequirements (PSBR) of 3.8 percent of GDP during 2021. Direct budgetary support in 2020 amounted to\n0.7 percent of GDP whereas guarantees and loans to MSMEs by development banks amounted to 1.2\npercent of GDP, focused on increased public health spending, small credits to MSMEs and the informal\nsector, and targeted social transfers to vulnerable groups. Mexico's overall PSBR were 4 percent of GDP\nin 2020, driven by a moderate increase in public spending and sustained revenues fueled by strong tax\nadministration measures and tax settlements with large companies. Yet, net public debt as a share of GDP\nincreased to 52.4 percent due to the fiscal deficit, debt reevaluation determined by foreign exchange\n(FOREX), and the large contraction in nominal GDP in 2020. The fiscal response during the pandemic, albeit\nat the risk of a deeper contraction and the sharp depreciation led to an increase in public indebtedness,\nbelow however to what was observed in other emerging markets. The 2021 fiscal situation benefited from\nthe economic recovery and higher oil prices, an expenditure that continues part of the pandemic fiscal\nstimulus centered on public health and social transfers and implementing large public investment\nprojects.\n14. Petróleos Mexicanos (PEMEX) has continued to report losses, equivalent to MXN 224 billion\n(US$10.9 billion) in 2021. Financial support from the Government during 2019–2021 (amounting to a\ncumulative 2.7 percent of 2021 GDP) and higher oil prices mitigated these losses. Federal government\nsupport to PEMEX came in the form of tax relief (US$12.7 billion), financing debt repayments (US$10\nbillion), and direct funding for investment projects (US$6.5 billion), and cash support for other obligations\n(US$6.7 billion), totaling US$35.9 billion (2.7 percent of GDP)18. This financial support was aimed to\npartially compensate for the US$41.6 billion losses of 2019 and 2020. PEMEX’s financial debt remains high\nand total financial obligations reached MXN 2243 billion (US$109 billion) at the end of 2021. PEMEX is\nexpected to benefit from the higher oil prices as the Mexican-Mix oil price averaged US$35.7 per barrel in\n2020, to reach US$64.7 in 2021, while averaging almost US$90 per barrel in the first quarter of 2022.\nPEMEX's oil production declined from an average of over 2 million barrels per day during 2015-2017, to\naround 1.75 million barrels per day in 2021. The fiscal support improves some of PEMEX challenges related\nto significant tax burden and high financial cost. A turnaround of PEMEX finances would also require to\nprioritize profitable business areas by redirecting investment for replacing reserves, scaling down large\ninvestment projects and focusing on maintaining and upgrading existing refineries, stimulating synergies\nwith the private sector in exploration and oil production, and selling noncore assets. PEMEX remains three\nnotches below investment grade for Fitch and Moody’s after a downgrade by Moody’s by one notch in\nJuly 2021 (it is still investment grade for S&P). The fiscal support to PEMEX as well as its balance and debt\nare part of the fiscal account figures reported for the consolidated public sector (tables 1 and 2).\n15. The 2022 public budget approved aims to stabilize the overall fiscal deficit at 2.7 percent of GDP\nand stabilize the net public debt-to-GDP ratio at 49.6 percent over the medium term.19 On the\n18 Box 2. IMF, Mexico Article IV, November 2021.\n19 Considering the most updated fiscal scenario, Precriterios de política económica para el 2023.\nPage 14\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nexpenditure side, the budget envisions a re-prioritization of expenditures toward health (a 27.6 percent\nincrease in real terms compared to 2021) and social assistance programs (real increment of 12.8 percent)\nto vulnerable groups while keeping public investment at 3.1 percent of GDP, mainly for flagship projects.\nThe impact of the pandemic-induced economic downturn in 2020 on debt is not expected to affect the\ndebt consolidation process, based on a contained fiscal deficit under the long-standing prudent fiscal\npolicy of Mexico, further supported by the limited fiscal impulse to remove. The budget envisages\nincreases in public revenues driven by the recovery in economic activity and efforts to tackle elusion and\nevasion. Also, to simplify the tax filing process, reducing costs, and increasing the tax base, the new budget\nlaw adopts (a) a simplified tax filing regime for independent workers that limits deductions and (b) a\nspecial regime for small firms with accelerated depreciation to boost investment and employment.\nTable 1. Banxico has also updated its projections for annual inflation in 2022\nfrom 5.2 percent to 6.4 percent.\nFiscal stance. Mexico's fiscal position is expected to improve through increased oil revenues. However, to\nmitigate additional inflationary pressures, the government relies on the revenue increase to cut excise tax\non gas and lower the income tax and VAT on gasoline distributors. World Bank’s staff analysis projects\nthat the windfall is expected to increase PEMEX revenues by around 0.8 percent of GDP. The federal\ngovernment is expected to increase revenues by 0.3 percent of GDP with tax exemption cost estimated\nto be 0.6 percent of GDP, totaling a net cost of 0.25 percent of GDP for the federal government (and a 0.5\npercent net benefit for the public sector).\nPoverty and distribution. The cost of the basic food basket increased by 13.9 (12.8) percent in rural\n(urban) areas in the 12 months to end-February, significantly above the average food inflation observed\nin LAC (9 percent). Food expenditures represent more than 50 percent of total expenditures of the bottom\n15 percent of the distribution and more than 40 percent of total expenditures of the bottom 60 percent\nof the distribution. As such, the purchasing power of poor and vulnerable households will be affected,\npotentially leading to heightened food insecurity, particularly in urban areas, where the largest number\nof poor reside.\n2.3. IMF RELATIONS\n24. On November 3, 2021, the Executive Board of the IMF considered the staff report that\nconcluded the Article IV consultation with Mexico. A two-year precautionary FCL arrangement for SDR\n44.6 billion (about US$61 billion, at the time of signing) was approved on November 22, 2019. The\narrangement was renewed for two years to an initial amount equivalent to SDR 35.7 billion (about US$50\nbillion at the time of signing) that will be phased out at the request of the Mexican authorities as the risks\ncontinue to decline. The IMF and World Bank maintain close collaboration on macroeconomic and\nstructural issues.\n3. GOVERNMENT PROGRAM\n25. The National Development Plan (NDP) 2018–2024 includes the programs and public policies\nreaffirming the GoM’s commitment to sustainable development and maintaining a sustainable fiscal\nframework. The NDP seeks to relaunch economic growth, anchored on formal jobs creation, by boosting\nemployment generation, improving education, health, and the population's overall well-being. It also\npursues balancing economic growth with regional integration and regional economic convergence. The\nNDP emphasizes interventions that benefit the most vulnerable groups, particularly the poor and the\nunderserved population in rural areas, and policies and programs to achieve the government’s target of\nreducing poverty.\n26. As the government starts the second half of its six-year mandate, the 2022 public budget aims\nto consolidate the economic recovery following the impact of the pandemic by increasing and\nPage 22\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nredirecting expenditures towards health, social programs, sustainable rural development, and public\ninvestment in flagship projects. The 2022 public budget24 aims to prioritize and contain spending while\nincreasing revenue resulting from the economic recovery and growing tax collection, partly due to the\nsimplified tax regime supported by this operation, and higher oil prices. The budget's focus is to prop up\nthe economic recovery by boosting social protection and public health systems and increasing public\ninvestment in flagship projects, particularly in the South and Southeast regions of the country, to improve\njob creation, GDP growth and productivity convergence with the rest of the country. The government has\nalso adopted a new simplified regime for low-income earners to strengthen the tax authority to combat\nevasion and avoidance, a critical structural challenge in Mexico. The reform is designed to be revenue-\nneutral in the short term to support the economic recovery and positively impact revenues in the medium\nterm once the recovery occurs due to an increase in formalization resulting from decreasing compliance\ncosts. In addition, Mexico increased the environmental expense budget by 30 percent from 2021 to 2022,\nensuring that sustainable forest management and climate change adaptation policies are integrated into\nMexico’s efforts to boost economic growth, enhance income opportunities, and improve livelihoods for\nurban and rural populations.\n4. PROPOSED OPERATION\n4.1. LINK TO GOVERNMENT PROGRAM AND OPERATION DESCRIPTION\n27. The development objective of the proposed operation is to support policy measures of the\nauthorities that: (a) support vulnerable groups and regions to reap the benefits of economic recovery;\nand (b) reform key policies and institutions that enable sustainable economic growth.\n28. The operation is structured around two pillars. Pillar 1 supports: (i) adopting a new, simplified\nregime for MSMEs and self-employed individuals; and (ii) policies and institutional reforms to facilitate\nfinancial inclusion, particularly for Mexican migrants in the United States. Pillar 2 supports reforms that:\n(i) improve the management of climate-induce disasters; (ii) ensure sustainable management and\nconservation of forests; (iii) enhance conservation areas that mutually store carbon and improve\nresilience; and (iv) enhance the voice and agency of vulnerable rural populations around natural resource\nmanagement.\n29. The operation is aligned with the government program. The operation supports key priorities\nadopted by the government in the 2022 public budget, including the simplified tax regime and the\nfinancial mechanisms to ensure support in case of natural disasters. It also supports key government\npriorities, such as strengthening the forestry law to bring additional regulatory certainty and regulations\nto increase financial inclusion. These reforms are expected to benefit the poorest rural areas contributing\nto closing regional income gaps, one of the main objectives of the government as stated in the NDP.\n24\nhttps://www.finanzaspublicas.hacienda.gob.mx/work/models/Finanzas_Publicas/docs/paquete_economico/cgpe/cgpe_2022.p\ndf.\nPage 23\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\n30. The design of this DPL includes lessons learned from prior policy operations. Recent DPFs in\nMexico show that stand-alone DPFs provide the needed flexibility for supporting reforms led by the\nGovernment in stages, as it builds consensus along the process. DPFs in Mexico have proven more\nimpactful when complemented by a program of technical and analytical tools that help build support\naround evidence of the reforms and ensure that the World Bank's analytical work is closely aligned to\nMexico’s demands and reform momentum. The analytical underpinnings of this operation have been\nbased on the broad Technical assistance (TA) and analytical work carried out in the recent past. TA and\nanalytical work are expected to continue in order to support policy implementation under the operation.\n4.2. PRIOR ACTIONS, RESULTS AND ANALYTICAL UNDERPINNINGS\nPillar 1: Inclusive economic growth\n31. Pillar 1 of the operation focuses on reforms supporting vulnerable groups and regions to better\nreap the benefits of economic growth, deepening reforms in long-standing areas of engagement with\nthe GoM. It will do so by (PA 1) simplifying the declaration and payment of taxes for MSMEs, (PA 2)\nfacilitating the identity verification of Mexicans in the US to open bank accounts in Mexico, and (PA 3)\nexpanding the supply of financial services through electronic payment systems.\nPrior Action 1: : The Borrower has enacted an amendment to the Income Tax Law to: (a) strengthen the\ntax framework by closing loopholes for tax evasion and avoidance, providing the tax authority with new\ntools to combat tax evasion and avoidance, including in mergers, acquisitions and restructurings; and (b)\nintroduce new simplified regimes for enterprises with income up to an amount of 35 million Mexican Pesos\nand self-employed, reducing the number of procedures to declare and pay taxes, as evidenced,\nrespectively, by the following provisions under the amendment to the Income Tax Law published in the\nOfficial Gazette on November 12, 2021: (i) amendments to Articles 24 and 161 of the Income Tax Law; and\n(ii) introduction of articles 113-E to 113-J under Title IV, Chapter II, Section IV, called ‘Simplified Trust\nRegime’, and Articles 206 to 215 under Title VII, Chapter XII, called “Simplified Trust Regime for legal\npersons” in the Income Tax Law.\n32. Rationale. Mexico has multiple tax regimes with significant administrative burdens that deter\nformalization, especially for small taxpayers. In Mexico, enterprises require 241 hours a year on average\nto comply with their tax obligations, compared to 161 hours in OECD member countries. Furthermore,\nthe Tax Incorporation Regime (Régimen de Incorporación Fiscal, RIF) introduced in 2014 was not effective\nin increasing formality or helping business growth. There are 2.1 million taxpayers, individuals, and micro\nand small establishments in Mexico subject to the same obligations as large companies, raising\nadministrative costs. As a result, tax avoidance and evasion are widespread in Mexico. Several studies\nshow that tax evasion is estimated to be between 2.6 and 6.2 percent of GDP.25\n33. Substance of the prior action. The reform aims to reduce the procedures and time allocated to\ndeclare and pay taxes in Mexico to increase tax compliance and facilitate formalization. Implementing\nmeasures to reduce informality has become particularly urgent as the share of informal workers has\nincreased. The reform seeks to reduce the cost of compliance for taxpayers with less administrative\ncapacity. The new simplified regime aims to maintain similar effective rates for income tax for individuals\n25 Buehn and Schneider 2016; San Martin et al. 2017.\nPage 24\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nwith lower incomes, calibrating the reform to ensure its revenue-neutrality in the short term. The new\nregime allows for the auto-population of the monthly tax returns based on e-invoices, significantly\nsimplifying tax filing for small and micro enterprises. Informality will be deterred by simplifying the\ncalculation, reducing the amount of taxes, and fighting the use of invoices—both false and real—currently\nused to deduct taxes. The reform simplifies the tax regimes for MSMEs and self-employed, and grants,\naccelerated depreciation to support investment. The RIF’s new simplified system for income tax extends\nto a broader set of taxpayers (i.e., professionals) for individuals with a gross income of up to MXN 3.5\nmillion per year (around US$170,000) and legal entities with an income of up to MXN 35 million annual\ngross income (around US$1.7 million). The new regime simplifies compliance with tax obligations,\npreventing the taxpayer from keeping all the digital tax receipts or invoices, since the tax collection will\nbe calculated directly on the total income.\n34. Expected results. The reform is designed to be revenue-neutral in the short term to support the\neconomy's recovery. Still, it is expected to raise revenues once the economic recovery is well established,\npartly due to the increase in formalization associated with the reduction in compliance costs.26\nInternational experience suggests that revenue gains can be large; for instance, in India, Argentina,\nSwitzerland, Chile, and Canada, similar reforms increased 15–30 percent in the taxpayer base over two to\nthree years. The indicator proposed tracks the reform's impact during the timeline of the operation and\nreduces the number of procedures to comply with tax payment obligations from 8 or 10 in 2021 to 6 in\n2022, depending on the taxpayer´s activity, therefore the net reduction in the tax procedures would be\nequivalent to 2 or 4, respectively. The new regime is expected to increase the taxpayer base above GDP\ngrowth in the medium to long term.27 Also, the reform will improve tax administration by strengthening\ntax enforcement and simplifying tax compliance. The SAT (Mexico’s Internal Revenue Service) will have\nnew monitoring tools to combat tax evasion and avoidance, among others, during mergers, acquisitions,\nand restructurings.28 Finally, by reducing informality in the long term, this reform will also lead to higher\nsocial security contributions.\nPrior Action 2: The Borrower's Securities and Banking Commission has amended provisions applicable to\ncredit institutions to facilitate financial inclusion for Mexican migrants in the United States and improve\nthe safety and affordability of remittances flows by enabling them to remotely open transaction accounts\nwith Mexican development and commercial banks and including additional credentials to be used as valid\ndocuments to prove their identity, as evidenced by the resolution issued by the Borrower’s Securities and\nBanking Commission amending general provisions applicable to credit institutions, published in the Official\nGazette on May 21, 2021.\n26 Materializing the full impact of this reform may require policy makers to act on several fronts at the same time, including\nsimplifying related areas (i.e. labor market regulations) in order to tilt the cost–benefit analysis of firms toward formality by\ncombining both positive and negative incentives.\n27 Most of these will come from a reduction of tax expenditures associated with discounts of personal income taxes, value added\ntax (VAT), and the special tax on production and services (IEPS) benefiting taxpayers currently filing taxes under the RIF, which\nrepresented MXN 13.5 billion (MMP) in 2021. The approved modifications of personal deduction due to donations, which\naccounted for a MXN 1.1 billion in 2021, are also expected to be reduced by around 30 percent.\n28 For example, in case of identifying a corporate restructuring or a merger whose only reason is the reduction of tax obligations\n(without a business reason), the legal classification could be changed to an alienation of goods. Also, the reform modifies\nobligations for natural and legal persons residing abroad. Among these is the obligation to present information on transfer prices\nto people with a source of wealth located in the national territory.\nPage 25", "excerpt_sha256": "1554544bd99c10562afa12873b9f0f4448cca2aa1f5b529a782461ca7416b6c7", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["Removed unsupported claims about neutral poverty and social effects, consumer protection, and cybersecurity."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0014-E016", "source_id": "SRC-c0d225ca90a4346a", "url": "https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/556411654274244589/pdf/Mexico-Inclusive-and-Sustainable-Economic-Growth-Development-Policy-Loan.pdf", "title": "Mexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth Development Policy Loan", "publisher": "World Bank", "raw_sha256": "ec19b96f3781e708713ba3527f80235331cdc3ca896f3e637d27a75e528751d2", "fact_summary": "The government established budgetary mechanisms and risk-transfer provisions for post-disaster response, amended the Forest Law to require mitigation of lost carbon storage, prohibit land-use authorization on recently deforested land, and require free, prior, and informed consultation in Indigenous territories. It also declared the Sierra de San Miguelito protected area, covering 111,160 hectares and securing an estimated 89,362 hectares of ecosystems storing about 1.1 million tons of carbon. The World Bank identified positive environmental and resilience objectives but noted implementation and distributional risks.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "3B.9", "5B.8", "5B.9", "6B.3", "6B.6", "6B.8", "6B.9", "8B.2", "8B.5", "8B.6"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "The legal reforms and protected-area decree were adopted in 2021, with implementation and results targets extending to 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The measures were enacted by the López Obrador government through SHCP, SEMARNAT, and presidential decree.", "limitations": ["The document does not verify full implementation or realized 2023 outcomes."], "start_unit": 34, "end_unit": 39, "locator": "page 34 through page 39", "exact_excerpt": "The World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nthe average gender gap in ownership of various financial products (percentage points) (baseline: 7.4\npercentage points [2018]; target: 0 [December 2023])44.\nPillar 2: Sustainable economic growth\n45. The policy reforms supported by this operation show a progressive ambition of Mexico’s\nsustainable growth policies. Pillar 2 of the operation supports reforms that foster sustainability and\nresilience, addressing risks to people and the economy in an integrated manner, in line with the World\nBank GRID framework. Supported reforms include new regulatory and policy instruments that tackle\ndeforestation (PA 6) and mitigate CO emissions from land-use change (PA 5). They also reduce the\n2\nvulnerability of the population most exposed to climate-induced impacts through enhanced disaster risk\nmanagement (PA 4) and expansion of NPAs to improve climate change adaptation (PA 8). They also\nenhance the voice of IPs and those underrepresented, specifically women (PA 7).\nPrior Action 4: The Borrower has taken measures to strengthen its capacity to rapidly mobilize and target\nfunds post-disaster by establishing provisions detailing budgetary mechanisms for financing projects and\nprograms to respond to natural emergencies and disasters, as well as promoting the use of financing and\nrisk transfer mechanisms to complement budgetary mechanisms, as evidenced by the specific provisions\nthat establish the budgetary mechanisms to execute programs and projects to address the damages from\nnatural phenomena issued by the Head of the Policy and Budgetary Control Unit of the SHCP, published in\nthe Official Gazette on November 23, 2021.45\n46. Rationale. Mexico is highly exposed to natural hazards and climate risks and has historically been\na global champion and standard setter for proactive, data-driven approaches for identifying and managing\nclimate and disaster risks and impacts.46 In addition, effective disaster risk management is an integral part\nof countries as they pursue sustainable, resilient economic growth. Enhancing Mexico’s capacity to\nmanage disasters is also required to enhance the country’s adaptation capacities. Effectively managing\nemergencies after disasters will significantly enhance Mexico’s capacity to address disaster impacts and\nplan better for future scenarios, with important benefits for the country’s economic growth strategies in\nlight of increasing climate impacts. For that reason, Article 37 of the Federal Law of Budget and Fiscal\nResponsibility (Ley Federal de Presupuesto y Responsabilidad Hacendaria) establishes that the federal\nbudget must include provisions for preventive actions or execute programs and projects to address the\ndamages provoked by natural disasters. Budget availability and clear processes to dispose of and manage\nthat budget are key aspects of public policies to reduce the vulnerability of local populations to climate\nchange effects.\n44 The baseline for both indicators will be updated during the supervision of the operation to include the results from the\nfinancial inclusion survey that will be released in mid-2022.\n45 https://www.dof.gob.mx/nota_detalle.php?codigo=5636005&fecha=23/11/2021.\n46 The backbone of Mexico’s disaster risk management system is the legal, regulatory, and operational framework shared between\nthe Secretariat of Security and Civil Protection (Secretaría de Seguridad y Protección Ciudadana, SSPC) and SHCP on financial and\noperational aspects of disaster management.\nPage 30\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\n47. Furthermore, the budget assigned for 2022 for emergency-related expenditures only covers\nfinancing needs for an average (or below average) year in terms of annual losses. Mexico is currently quite\nfinancially exposed to another high-impact event like the 2017 earthquake. An event of this scale would\nexceed the 2022 budget and, in the absence of alternative risk financing arrangements, would negatively\nimpact other development programs’ budgets. Since the abrogation of the Mexico’s National Disaster\nFund (FONDEN), the Ministry of Security and Civil Protection (SSPC) has clarified operational aspects,\nincluding assessing damages. There was the risk that without clarity on assessing damages and budgetary\nmechanisms, in the event of a disaster, public financial resources could not be deployed or be done with\ndelay.\n48. Substance of the prior action. The provisions published by the Ministry of Finance and Public\nCredit (SHCP) under the Federal Law on Budget and Financial Responsibility establish budgetary\nmechanisms for financing emergency response and reconstruction (housing and public infrastructure)\nprojects and programs. They clarify the procedures for line ministries to request resources following the\nDeclaration of a Natural Disaster by SSPC or a corresponding declaration of health emergency by the\nGeneral Health Council. These new provisions are a tool to guarantee the Government’s timely response\nto climate-induced natural disasters to assist affected populations, especially the most vulnerable, by\ndeploying financial resources. The provisions also cover procurement of insurance and risk transfer\ninstruments for damage caused by natural phenomena and further specify that these schemes must be\ncontracted to protect the public balance sheet under the leadership of the Insurance, Pensions, and Social\nSecurity Unit (Unidad de Seguros, Pensiones y Seguridad Social - USPSS) of SHCP. USPSS would be\nresponsible for developing schemes to define the details of the Government’s disaster risk financing\nstrategy going forward. Thus, the new budgetary provisions also enable Mexico to better establish an\neffective response to climate-linked natural disasters.\n49. Expected results. The prior action is expected to facilitate the flow of resources to sectors and\nstates affected by climate-induced natural disasters, particularly the southern states, that are more\nvulnerable to climate-induced natural hazards and have less financial space to address them. It is also\nexpected to promote internal institutional collaboration within the Ministry of Finance on the interface\nbetween prearranged budgetary and financial mechanisms to prepare for disaster and climate shocks, for\nexample, procurement of disaster insurance schemes. The results will be measured by the number of risk\nfinancing instruments internally requested and supported by sound technical analysis. These instruments\nwill be selected and ultimately designed based on commercially-credible actuarial analysis to support\noptimal cost efficiency and coverage. This result will ultimately enable Mexico to strengthen its capacity\nto better prepare for future climate-induced external shocks and integrate disaster risk management\nbudget planning as an essential element to the country’s long-term adaptation planning.\nPrior Action 5: The Borrower has enacted an amendment to the Forest Law to establish that SEMARNAT\ncan only authorize land use change in forest lands for projects that, among others, mitigate the loss of\nforests’ carbon storage capacity, as evidenced by the amendment to the first paragraph of Article 93 of\nthe Forest Law published in the Official Gazette on April 26, 2021.\nPage 31\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\n50. Rationale. Regulating land use change is an integral tool for sustainable development as it\nestablishes the conditions under which natural vegetation, including forests, can be removed to facilitate\ninfrastructure development. In Mexico, the forest legal framework regulates the mitigation and\ncompensation of the environmental impacts of these land use change activities. Removal of forest\nvegetation is prohibited except if the proposed project obtains a land use change authorization from\nSEMARNAT, which needs to verify that biodiversity will be maintained and how the impacts on water and\nsoils will be mitigated. These projects must also contribute to CONAFOR’s ‘Fondo Forestal Mexicano’ to\nrestore forests in other areas of the same watershed where the land use change occurred. However,\nbefore 2021, the law did not consider measures to demonstrate that the loss in carbon storage capacity\nwould be mitigated, diminishing these ecosystems’ capacity to act as carbon sinks. Forests play a crucial\nrole in achieving Mexico’s NDC targets (the land use, land use change, and forestry [LULUCF] sector\nremovals are 163 MtCO per year, which is 20 percent of the country’s emissions). However, according to\n2\nMexico’s Forest Emissions Reference Level, the country emitted 20.2 MtCO e per year from 2007 to 2016\n2\ndue to deforestation and forest degradation. Only a portion of this deforestation is legal.\n51. Substance of the prior action. In 2021, the GoM amended Article 93 of the Forest Law to establish\nthat projects in forest lands seeking land use change authorization must mitigate their impacts on the loss\nof carbon stocks of forests. This will mitigate emissions from the LULUCF sector, which according to\nMexico’s updated NDCs in 2020 were 36 MtCO and are expected to further increase (in the business-as-\n2\nusual scenario) to 49 MtCO in 2030. Hence, this policy will help Mexico attain its NDC, which pledges a\n2\nreduction of 22 percent of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 2030.\n52. Expected results. It is expected that 100 percent of the land use change authorizations in forest\nlands will include mandatory measures to mitigate the reduction of carbon storage capacity. In addition,\nat project closure it will be possible to quantify the reduction of stored carbon that was avoided through\nthese measures.47 Without this prior action, the carbon stored in forests affected by land use changes\nwould have been released into the atmosphere (up to 0.88 million tCO2 based on the area that obtained\nland use change authorizations in previous years) as no mitigation measures were mandated by the Forest\nLaw before.\nPrior Action 6: The Borrower has enacted an amendment to the Forest Law to enhance forest protection\nby forbidding the issuance of land use change authorizations in forest lands that were\npreviously deforested, as evidenced by the amendment to Article 97 of the Forest Law dated April 20, 2021,\nand published in the Official Gazette on April 26, 2021.\n53. Rationale. Mexico lost almost 1 million ha of forests from 2014 to 2016 due to deforestation.48\nFirst, this generated GHG emissions of around 26 million tons of CO2 per year (around 3.2 percent of\nMexico’s yearly emissions). This is particularly important considering that Mexico’s forests currently\nrepresent a carbon sink (they remove more CO2 than they emit). However, the LULUCF sector generates\n4.5 percent of all emissions. Second, the loss of natural ecosystems particularly increases the vulnerability\nof marginalized communities to climate change and reduces their opportunities to generate sustainable\n47 A quantification at project preparation is not possible given the uncertainty on the number and scope of permits to be\nrequested. An ex-post analysis is feasible based on actual permits requested and endorsed.\n48 These are the most recent official data for deforestation: CONAFOR. 2020. Mexico’s proposed reference level. Available online\nat: https://redd.unfccc.int/files/nref_2007-2016_mexico.pdf.\nPage 32\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nrural local economies as they have a high dependency on natural phenomena.49 In Mexico, forests are\nmainly owned by communities.50 Around 3,520 of them have management plans for timber or non-\ntimber forest products51 that enable them to generate income and employment from its sustainable use\nand commercialization. Deforestation affects the possibilities of scaling up these initiatives to foster\nsustainable rural livelihoods. However, the efforts to halt deforestation are challenged by several negative\nincentives to illegally change land use,52 such as establishing high-value commercial crops or constructing\nthe infrastructure that can affect communities’ capacity for long-term sustainable development. Before\n2021, landowners could deforest their land expecting not to be detected, and if caught, they could pay\nthe fine and then ask for a land use change authorization to legalize the land use change53.\n54. Substance of the prior action. The law modification prevents the authorization of land use\nchanges in forest lands that were previously deforested, eliminating perverse incentives to deforestation\nand compelling land managers/owners to use the legal channels to ask for land use change authorizations\nbefore developing any project. When done through these legal channels, land use change projects must\nmitigate their environmental impacts and pay a compensation fee to CONAFOR’s ‘Fondo Forestal\nMexicano’ to be used in ecosystem restoration activities. The policy reform specifically prohibits land use\nchange authorization in forest lands for 20 years after the deforestation occurred and until the forest has\ncompletely regenerated, hereby recognizing the long-term value of forests to sustainable growth.\n55. Expected results. It is expected that 100 percent of the land-use change authorization requests\nin previously deforested will be denied authorization from SEMARNAT. This will directly contribute to\nMexico’s NDC target of zero net deforestation by removing some incentives to deforest illegally.\nPrior Action 7: The Borrower has enacted an amendment to the Forest Law to introduce free, prior, and\ninformed consultation for authorizing land use changes in forest lands that are located in indigenous\nterritories, as evidenced by the amendment to the second paragraph of Article 93 of the Forest Law\npublished in the Official Gazette on April 26, 2021.\n56. Rationale. IPs represent 21.5 percent of Mexico’s population (25.7 million).54 Historically, they are\namong Mexico's most vulnerable and marginalized population groups and suffer from exclusion to\nservices and markets in higher numbers than non-IPs. In 2020, 73.2 percent of IPs lived in poverty (29.2\npercent in extreme poverty), compared to 47.1 percent of the non-indigenous population (6.4 percent in\nextreme poverty). In addition, 19 percent of the municipalities highly vulnerable to climate change are\nclassified as indigenous municipalities, and the effect of climate change on the livelihoods of the most\nvulnerable is disproportionally high. Thus, both extreme climate and slow-onset climate change events,\nrepresent additional stressors that will most likely worsen the situation of vulnerable populations in the\ncountry. Article 27 of the Mexican Constitution establishes that the law must protect the integrity of the\nlands of the IPs, which are key for the conservation of their natural heritage and the creation of thriving\nand resilient livelihoods. Mexico has adopted legal and institutional instruments that recognize the IPs’\n49 World Bank. 2018. Mexico - Systematic Country Diagnostic (English). Washington, DC: World Bank Group.\n50 Madrid, L., Núñez, J. M., Quiroz, G., & Y., R. 2009. La propiedad social forestal en México. Investigación Ambiental. Instituto\nNacional de Ecología.\n51SEMARNAT. 2021. Anuario estadístico de la producción forestal 2018.\n52 CONAFOR. 2019. Estado que guarda el sector forestal en México.\n53 According to CONAFOR (2019), from December 2018 to June 2019, there were 383 inspections by PROFEPA to land use changes.\n54 INEGI’s Census 2020: https://www.inegi.org.mx/programas/ccpv/2020/.\nPage 33\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nrights to consultation regarding any legal or administrative government measure that may affect them.55\nHowever, before 2021, the environmental legal framework did not include the IPs’ consultation as part of\nthe processes of the main environmental administration procedures, such as the environmental impact\nevaluation or the land use change authorization. For that reason, this 2021 reform to the Forest Law\nstrengthens the environmental policy instruments for more inclusive and sustainable economic growth.\nThe aforementioned enhances the voice and agency of rural indigenous communities and enables an\ninclusive process within the country’s growth strategies.\n57. Substance of the prior action. In line with the constitutional rights of IPs in Mexico, in April 2021,\nthe borrower approved a reform to Article 93 of the General Law of Sustainable Forest Development\n(Forest Law),56 establishing that land use change authorizations in forest lands located in indigenous\nterritories must be accompanied by a free, prior, and informed consultation. For that, SEMARNAT must\ncoordinate with the National Institute of the Indigenous Peoples (Instituto Nacional de los Pueblos\nIndígenas), which is legally in charge57 of leading IPs’ consultations. Hence, no land use authorizations in\nforest lands may be granted in indigenous territories without this process. This participatory process in\nland use changes will lead to social inclusion in climate-relevant actions, empowering IPs and enhancing\nthe voice and agency over land use change processes in their territory and associated mitigation actions\nestablished as part of the authorization. This enables IPs to engage in decision-making over climate change\nsolutions on the ground.\n58. Expected results. The prior action will improve the right to information and self-determination of\nIPs as well as the right to preserve their territories, established in the Mexican Constitution and align with\nConvention No. 169 of the ILO. The results will be measured considering the percentage of land use\nchange authorizations in forest lands affecting indigenous territories that include a free, prior, and\ninformed consultation process. These consultations will ultimately enable rural indigenous communities\nto protect their territories and natural resources against external threats, which also enhances their\ncapacity to generate local income opportunities through natural resource management. In addition, as all\nconsultations under this policy reform relate to land use changes that if agreed include mandatory\nmitigation measures on biodiversity and carbon, among other, the reform also fosters local nature-based\nsolutions for climate change adaptation.\nPrior Action 8: The Borrower has declared a new natural protected area in the Sierra of San Miguelito to\nenhance protection of natural capital and climate change resilience, and foster sustainable development,\nas evidenced by the Presidential Decree published in the Official Gazette on December 13, 2021.\n59. Rationale. Mexico ratified the Paris Agreement in September 2016 and has pledged to reduce 22\npercent of its GHG emissions by 2030,58 including a specific objective of enhancing conservation,\n55 Mexico is a signatory party of the Indigenous and Tribal Peoples Convention (No. 169) of the International Labour Organization\n(ILO). Mexico has developed legal and public policy instruments, such as the Law of the National Institute of the Indigenous\nPeoples, the 2019 published protocol for the free, prior, and informed consultation of indigenous peoples and Afro-descendants\n(http://www.inpi.gob.mx/gobmx-2019/convocatorias/inpi-protocolo-consulta-reforma-constitucional-derechos-pueblos-\nindigenas.pdf), and the National Program for the Indigenous Peoples 2018–2024\n(https://www.gob.mx/cms/uploads/attachment/file/423227/Programa-Nacional-de-los-Pueblos-Indigenas-2018-2024.pdf).\n56 https://www.diputados.gob.mx/LeyesBiblio/pdf/LGDFS_260421.pdf.\n57 Law of the National Institute of the Indigenous Peoples: https://www.gob.mx/cms/uploads/attachment/file/421725/ley-INPI-\ndof-04-12-2018.pdf.\n58 2020 Update of Mexico’s NDCs.\nPage 34\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nrestoration, and sustainable use of biodiversity and ecosystem services. This objective addresses both\nclimate change mitigation and adaptation targets by recognizing the fundamental role of nature-based\nsolutions in enhancing resilience and natural ecosystems' capacity to store carbon. The objective is\nimplemented by expanding natural protected areas (NPAs) and strengthening their management and\nconnectivity, among other actions, as NPAs conserve forest carbon stocks, mitigate climate change, and\nincrease the resilience of local communities against the effects of climate change. To date, Mexico’s NPA\nsystem has a clear legal and institutional framework that effectively protects ecosystems. With this new\ndeclaration, Mexico now has 184 federal NPAs that account for 90.9 million ha, of which 21.48 million ha\nis terrestrial. NPAs are one of the main nature conservation instruments established in the Mexican\nenvironmental law. In the case of Sierra de San Miguelito this was threatened by the disorganized growth\nof urban areas of the capital city and extensive wildfires that had degraded a large area of pine forests. In\naddition, in and around the new NPA, various extractive activities threatened the natural ecosystem,\nresulting in the loss of critical forest coverage.59\n60. Substance of the prior action. The declaration of ‘Sierra de San Miguelito’ as a natural protected\narea provides a protection status under the Mexican environmental law to 111,160 ha. This will benefit\naround 1 million people living in and around the broader NPA through critical ecosystem services.60 The\nNPA is also the habitat of 700 vegetation and 300 animal species, 50 of which are under threat. The decree\n(a) forbids land-use change, mining, and other extractive activities in the ‘core zone’ of the NPA (24,516\nha); (b) restricts potentially harming activities in the ‘buffer zone’ (85,122 ha); and (c) regulates how to\nsustainably manage the ecosystems and natural resources in the NPA sustainably. The type of NPA\nselected allows for a broad variety of sustainable natural resource management and development\nactivities for local inhabitants, complemented by governmental support programs to develop economic\nactivities linked to conservation.\n61. Expected results. The prior action will ensure the conservation of 89,362 hectares of ecosystems\nthat store approximately 1.1 million tons of carbon, equivalent to 4.2 million tons of CO .61 The indicator\n2\nrecognizes the natural protected areas’ carbon capture capacity. In absence of the NPA decree, this\ncapacity to store carbon was at risk, given important drivers of deforestation in the area and expansion of\nurban centers. Through the decree, carbon capture capacity has been secured. This will directly benefit\nMexico’s NDC, that includes a zero net deforestation target and largely depends on enhanced carbon sinks\nto achieving its mitigation targets. The newly declared area will also enhance adaptative capacity of local\npopulations through enhanced natural resources management.\nTable 4. Given that the proposed area includes multiple zones that\nwould allow for some productive activities, the short-run poverty and social impacts are expected to be\nneutral.\n5.2. ENVIRONMENTAL, FORESTS, AND OTHER NATURAL RESOURCE ASPECTS\n76. Policy measures supported by the DPL are expected to have significant positive effects on\nclimate change and the environment. The WBG’s environmental analysis of the DPL finds that most\npolicies supported, mainly under pillar 2, would have significant positive effects on Mexico’s climate\nchange mitigation and environmental priorities. Policy measures of pillar 2 will enhance the\nenvironmental and climate change resilience under existing laws and programs that promote\nenvironmental sustainability and disaster risk management. Mexico’s institutional and regulatory\nframework to avoid, reduce, mitigate, and manage adverse environmental effects is sufficiently robust\nand can address potential negative environmental effects arising from these policy reforms.\n77. The policy measures will support Mexico in complying with its NDC by fostering low carbon and\nclimate-resilient growth, enhancing NPA management and forest conservation, and establishing\neffective disaster risk management. These policy measures will specifically strengthen the country’s\nadaptation capacity, particularly in the most vulnerable municipalities, and enhance resilience of urban\nand rural landscapes. The DPL will foster mandatory mitigation measures for land use changes in forest\nlands (PA 5), enforce measures to avoid deforestation (PA 6), and enhance Mexico’s area under\nconservation status (PA 8), contributing to the preservation of carbon sinks that are essential for Mexico\nto meet its NDC. The DPL will also strengthen disaster risk management (PA 4) which is expected to have\npositive environmental effects by fostering enhanced resilience and disaster responsiveness nationally.\nPolicy reforms under pillar 1 around digital financial inclusion could have longer-term positive\nenvironmental effects.\n78. Mexico has a solid and comprehensive national environmental legislation to promote\nsustainability and resilience measures supported by this operation. The legislation was formally\ndeveloped based on the General Law of Ecological Equilibrium and Environmental Protection (LGEEPA) of\n1988. Since then, Mexico has established laws, policies, programs, and provided resources that set\nenvironmental requirements to investments in terms of managing environmental impacts and waste,\ncontrolling pollution, and addressing material efficiency and climate change, among other. Regarding the\nlatter, the General Climate Change Law of 2012 supports the transition to a competitive, sustainable, and\nlow-carbon economy; reduces the vulnerability of the population and ecosystems; and assigns\nresponsibilities within the Government. The GoM has committed to supporting climate change mitigation\nand adaptation as reflected in its updated NDC under the United Nations Framework Convention on\nClimate Change.\n79. On November 22, 2021, the GoM issued a Presidential Accord declaring the execution of certain\ninfrastructure projects a matter of national security and public interest and ordered federal agencies to\nPage 41\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nissue provisional environmental and social permits within five business days from the time of the\nrequest, to expedite the execution of said projects. On December 14, 2021, the Mexican Supreme Court\npartially suspended the Presidential Accord in response to the constitutional controversy raised by the\nNational Institute of Transparency, Access to Information, and Protection of Personal Data (INAI) with\nrespect to any effects and consequences of the Presidential Accord that may derive from the classification\nof information as of public interest and/or national security. These legal challenges may take up to a year\nto be resolved, and project-specific provisional permits issued could be challenged by affected parties in\ncourts, seeking temporary suspension of the provisions of the Accord, or an injunction on project-specific\nrelated activities, until courts make a final decision. SEMARNAT has informed that, so far, the Accord has\nnot been implemented since approval and that it continues to apply Mexico’s environmental management\nframework, confirming that all environmental legislation remains valid and in place, and would be\nenforced during the operation’s timeline. However, the potential implementation of the Accord poses a\nsignificant risk that could weaken Mexico’s environmental management framework and undermine the\npolicy measures supported by the DPL under Pillar 2 (see paragraph 95).\n5.3. PFM, DISBURSEMENT AND AUDITING ASPECTS\n80. Mexico’s Public Financial Management (PFM) framework is mostly aligned with good\ninternational practices, including the use of budget resources and the FOREX internal control system\nmanaged by the Mexican Central Bank. According to the latest Fiscal Transparency Evaluation (FTE) of\nthe IMF (dated October 2018), Mexico scores relatively well compared to other Latin American countries\nand emerging market economies that have undergone such evaluations. Out of the 48 principles across\nfour pillars in the FTE, Mexico meets 16 principles at the basic level, 9 principles at the good level, and 15\nprinciples at the advanced level. The areas of strength include fiscal reporting, forecasting, and budgeting\nand resource revenue management. Conversely, the areas for improvement include the need to\nstrengthen the medium-term fiscal framework, the medium-term budget framework, and fiscal risk\nanalysis and management.\n81. Government commitment to support PFM reforms. Mexico’s budget legal and control\nframework is comprehensive, and the country has demonstrated a good track record in implementing\nPFM reforms. One of the recent major reforms focused on public sector accountability, integrity, and\ntransparency with the establishment of the Law63 of the National Anti-Corruption System, which aims to\nimprove coordination among different oversight entities and civil society organizations.64 However,\ncorruption control is an ongoing concern in the public sector.65 In addition, Mexico adopted accrual\naccounting, harmonizing accounting standards across all levels of government through the creation of the\nNational Council of Accounting Harmonization (Consejo Nacional de Armonización Contable) and the\nenactment of the General Government Accounting Law in 2008.\n63 The Law of the National Anti-Corruption System was issued in 2016 and reformed in 2021.\n64 The members of the National Anti-Corruption System include representatives from civil society, the heads of the Supreme\nAudit Institution, the Ministry of Public Administration, the anti-corruption agency, and INAI, among others.\n65 Mexico scored 31 out of 100 in the Corruption Perception Index prepared by Transparency International, below most of its\nregional peers.\nPage 42", "excerpt_sha256": "8f3219121f206a4e2f33d1ec53beda284251f05a8f5cc259b24de8301d435fa1", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0015-E003", "source_id": "SRC-74b9009ff2271f55", "url": "https://www.cndh.org.mx/sites/default/files/documentos/2023-02/Informe_Especial_SNDIFY_SDIF.pdf", "title": "Informe Especial sobre SNDIF y sistemas DIF", "publisher": "Comisión Nacional de los Derechos Humanos", "raw_sha256": "dbebd81a9400c0ab5432d82f368969cc1e42f91eceebcd881f1903c8e2b4899b", "fact_summary": "CNDH recommendations documented repeated violations involving migrants and children: deportation of Haitian refugee applicants without non-refoulement assessment; Los elementos de la\nGN incurrieron en violación al derecho de las niñas y niños involucrados a\nque se protegiera su integridad produciendo como consecuencia cualquier\ndaño físico o mental en ellos teniendo la obligación de brindarles\nprotección, por lo que los agentes omitieron tomar en consideración el\ninterés superior de la niñez.\n\n\n\n\n63\n  SCJN, Protocolo de Actuación para quienes imparten justicia en casos que involucren a\npersonas migrantes y sujetas de protección internacional, 2ª ed., México, SCJN, noviembre\n2015,      p.     13. Al respecto este Organismo Nacional había solicitado al INM,\nSEDENA y GN, entre otras autoridades la implementación de medidas\ncautelares a favor de ese grupo de personas que transitaba por el estado\nde Chiapas para que se efectuaran acciones dirigidas a su atención\nhumanitaria. Resultó inobjetable que el personal del INM había\ntransgredido los derechos humanos de las personas menores de edad que\nformaban parte de la Caravana en virtud de que agentes federales de\nMigración, hicieron uso desproporcionado de la fuerza, acciones y\nomisiones que pusieron en grave peligro la integridad y seguridad personal\nde 4 de ellos.\n\nRecomendación 2/2022.66 Respecto a personas en contexto de migración\nalojadas en la Estación Migratoria de Palenque, Chiapas, donde se advirtió\nla presencia de cuatro adolescentes no acompañados, los que enfermaron\nde COVID-19, sin haberlos canalizados a un lugar especializado para su\natención, sin haber acatado las medidas de prevención y protección para\nevitar contagios. Además de haberse advertido el empleo del uso de la fuerza por\npersonal del INM, se transgredió el principio del interés superior de la\nniñez, ya que al momento de que los padres fueron sujetos de la inspección\nmigratoria y de las agresiones físicas, su hija se encontraba con ellos,\nsituación que generó un sentimiento de miedo.\n\nEsta Comisión Nacional ha apuntado que como principio rector el interés\nsuperior de la niñez implica no sólo el reconocimiento de un criterio\nprioritario para la salvaguarda de los derechos, pues antes de ser personas\nen contexto de migración, son niñas, niños y adolescentes a quienes, de\nconformidad con el artículo 2 de la LGDNNA, se les debe considerar “de\nmanera primordial en la toma de decisiones sobre una cuestión debatida\nque los involucre. Cuando se presenten diferentes interpretaciones, se elegirá\nla que satisfaga de manera más efectiva” sus derechos en cuanto a su\ninterés superior.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                 41", "excerpt_sha256": "01337c6a0843143c8c2b7073345c738a61cdb4265a660c63c72286997c832ef9", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0015-E004", "source_id": "SRC-74b9009ff2271f55", "url": "https://www.cndh.org.mx/sites/default/files/documentos/2023-02/Informe_Especial_SNDIFY_SDIF.pdf", "title": "Informe Especial sobre SNDIF y sistemas DIF", "publisher": "Comisión Nacional de los Derechos Humanos", "raw_sha256": "dbebd81a9400c0ab5432d82f368969cc1e42f91eceebcd881f1903c8e2b4899b", "fact_summary": "Mexico established a 2021-2024 national child-protection program, a model assigning SNDIF and protection procuracies responsibility for migrant children, open or non-detention social assistance centers, multidisciplinary procedures, humanitarian visitor status, protection plans, interagency coordination, and a national mobility registry launched in October 2022 with gradual national implementation and international support.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.4", "3B.5", "3B.7", "6B.1", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.4", "6B.9", "8B.1", "8B.2", "8B.5", "8B.6"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "The institutional measures were adopted before or during 2022 and could operate in 2023;
